===== page 7 =====
Measure Theory and Integration By and For the
Learner
===== page 8 =====
Abstract.
Measure Theory and Integration is exposed with the clear aim to help begin-
ning learners to perfectly master its essence.
In opposition of a delivery of
the contents in an academic and vertical course, the knowledge is broken into
exercises which are left to the learners for solutions. Hints are present at any
corner to help readers to achieve the solutions. In that way, the knowledge
is constructed by the readers by summarizing the results of one or a group of
exercises.
Each chapter is organized into Summary documents which contain the knowl-
edge, Discovery documents which give the learner the opportunity to extract
the knowledge himself through exercises and into Solution Documents which
oﬀer detailed answers for the exercises. Exceptionally, a few number of re-
sults (A key lemma related the justiﬁcation of deﬁnition of the integral of a
non-negative function, the Caratheodory’s theorem and the Lebesgue-Stieljes
measure on Rd) are presented in appendix documents and given for reading in
small groups.
The full theory is presented in the described way. We highly expect that any
student who goes through the materials, alone or in a small group or under
the supervision of an assistant will gain a very solid knowledge in the subject
and by the way ensure a sound foundation for studying disciplines such as
Probability Theory, Statistics, Functional Analysis, etc.
The materials have been successfully used as such in normal real analysis
classes several times.
Keywords Sets, sets operations, measurable sets; measurable applications;
Borel sets; Borel Applications; measures; Lebesgue-Stieljes Measures; Count-
ing measures; ﬁnite products measures; ﬁnite and sigma-ﬁnite measures; ex-
tension of measures; Caratheodory’s Theorem; Convergence theorems; almost-
sure convergence; convergence in measure; integration; Riemann-Stieljes in-
tegration; Lebesgue-Stieljes integration; series and integrals with respect to
counting measures; Continuity and diﬀerentiability of integral depending on
continuous parameter; Fubini’s and Tonelli’s Theorem; Hahn-Jordan decom-
position Theorem; Lebesgue Decomposition Theorem; Radon-Nikodym’s The-
orem; High dimensional Lebesgue-Stieljes Integrals. Lp spaces.
AMS 2010 Classiﬁcation Subjects : 28A05; 28A10; 28A12; 28A20; 28A25;
28A33; 28A35

===== page 11 =====
General Preface
This textbook is one of the elements of a series whose ambition is to
cover a broad part of Probability Theory and Statistics. These text-
books are intended to help learners and readers, of all levels, to train
themselves.
As well, they may constitute helpful documents for professors and
teachers for both courses and exercises. For more ambitious people,
they are only starting points towards more advanced and personalized
books. So, these textbooks are kindly put at the disposal of professors
and learners.
Our textbooks are classiﬁed into categories.
A series of introductory books for beginners. Books of this se-
ries are usually destined to students of ﬁrst year in universities and to
any individual wishing to have an initiation on the subject. They do
not require advanced mathematics. Books on elementary probability
theory (See Lo (2017), for instance) and descriptive statistics are to be
put in that category. Books of that kind are usually introductions to
more advanced and mathematical versions of the same theory. Books
of the ﬁrst kind also prepare the applications of those of the second.
A series of books oriented to applications. Students or researchers
in very related disciplines such as Health studies, Hydrology, Finance,
Economics, etc. may be in need of Probability Theory or Statistics.
They are not interested in these disciplines by themselves.
Rather,
they need to apply their ﬁndings as tools to solve their speciﬁc prob-
lems. So, adapted books on Probability Theory and Statistics may be
composed to focus on the applications of such ﬁelds. A perfect example
concerns the need of mathematical statistics for economists who do not
necessarily have a good background in Measure Theory.
A series of specialized books on Probability theory and Sta-
tistics of high level.
This series begins with a book on Measure
1
===== page 12 =====
2
GENERAL PREFACE
Theory, a book on its probability theory version, and an introductory
book on topology. On that basis, we will have, as much as possible, a
coherent presentation of branches of Probability theory and Statistics.
We will try to have a self-contained approach, as much as possible, so
that anything we need will be in the series.
Finally, a series of research monographs closes this architecture.
This architecture should be so diversiﬁed and deep that the readers of
monograph booklets will ﬁnd all needed theories and inputs in it.
We conclude by saying that, with only an undergraduate level, the
reader will open the door of anything in Probability theory and sta-
tistics with Measure Theory and integration. Once this course
validated, eventually combined with two solid courses on topology and
functional analysis, he will have all the means to get specialized in any
branch in these disciplines.
Our collaborators and former students are invited to make live this
trend and to develop it so that the center of Saint-Louis becomes or
continues to be a re-known mathematical school, especially in Proba-
bility Theory and Statistics.
===== page 13 =====
Introduction
Measure Theory and Integration.
Undoubtedly, Measure Theory and Integration is one of the most im-
portant part of Modern Analysis, with Topology and Functional Anal-
ysis for example. Indeed, Modern mathematics is based on functional
analysis, which is a combination of the Theory of Measure and Inte-
gration, and Topology.
The application of mathematics is very pronounced in many ﬁelds,
such as ﬁnance (through stochastic calculus), mathematical economics
(through stochastic calculus), econometrics, which is a contextualiza-
tion of statistical regression to economic problems, physic statistics.
Probability Theory and Statistics has become an important tool for
the analysis of biological phenomena and genetics.
In all these cases, a solid basis in probability and mathematical statis-
tics is required. This can be achieved only with Measure theory and
integration through its powerful theorems of convergence, diﬀerentia-
tion and integrability.
The pedagogical orientation and organization of the contents.
In this book, the contents of Measure Theory and Integration are pre-
sented to the learners as solutions of exercises they are asked to solve.
The opportunity of extracting the knowledge themselves is given to
the learner. Undoubtedly, this makes the learner feel more conﬁdent in
himself, makes him fully participate in the classes and leads to a great
impact of the course on his level.
This method is used with the clear aim to help to beginning learners to
perfectly master the essence of the theory. In opposition of a delivery
of the contents in an academic and vertical course, the knowledge is
broken into exercises which are left to the learners for solutions. Hints
3
===== page 14 =====
4
INTRODUCTION
are present at any corner to help readers to achieve the solutions. In
that way, the knowledge is constructed by the readers by summarizing
the results of one or a group of exercises.
Let us ﬁrst describe how the book is organized. We tried to present all
the chapters in the same way.
(a) First, we give one, two or three documents in which the knowledge
is summarized. The learner is recommended to know the statements
in these summary as best as possible.
(b) Second, we put the knowledge in summary documents as conclu-
sions of a series or series of exercises. This means that we allow the
learner to discover himself the the elements of the courses.
These discovery exercises are accompanied with all the hints that allow
the learner to proceed alone, or in a small group.
(c) If we face a very heavy material (in length or in complexity), we
prefer to give it in an Appendix. The number of such documents does
not exceed three in the whole courses.
An assistant may be appointed to accompany small groups of learners
to read and repeat the proofs in these Appendix texts.
(d) The solutions of the discovery exercises are ﬁnally given. In these
documents, the exercises are listed in the same order and the solution
of each exercise is put just at its end. In general, the learner will see
that all the reasoning has already been given as hints.
At the beginning of each chapter, a table will present the documents
with the clear indications of the type : S for Summary, D for Discover
Exercises, SD for Solutions of the Discovery Exercises, A for Appendix.
So, each chapter must have at least three documents.
These documents of this book my be used as class materials, allowing
the teacher to let learners advancing at their own pace and next to help
them to get the knowledge.
Strongly motivated students are free to go faster and to ﬁnish the course
in a short time. But they are recommended to attend the classes and
===== page 15 =====
INTRODUCTION
5
to participate in the exchanges.
It is important to notice that the textbook begins by Chapter 0. This
means that all the content of that chapter is supposed to be known in
the former courses of General Algebra in the ﬁrst year of University.
But, we need them as the basis of the whole theory. We exposed it se-
riously as the other chapters by supposing that the reader might have
forgotten them.
A parallel textbook entirely devoted to advanced exercises and using
the knowledge developed in this book will be available in a recent fu-
ture.
We hope that the learners will proﬁt of this way of teaching measure
Theory and Integration, and will have the strongest basis to advance
to probability theory and Functional Analysis.
References.
Personally, my ﬁrst contact with the subject happened in the Integra-
tion course in 1983 in Dakar University with professor Sakhir Thiam.
The course was based on the Daniel integral (see Doc 09-04 and Doc
09-05, pages 648 and 654, for an introduction to that approach) and
some elements of Modern measure Theorem from the books of Genet
(1976) on Measure and Integration M´etivier (1979) on Probability The-
ory. That approach heavily depends on topological structures, mainly
on locally compact spaces.
During my graduate studies in Paris (1983-1986) I discovered the nice
book of Lo`eve (1997) and I have never stopped using it. Although
the book is the result of more than two decades of our teaching, it is
remains greatly inﬂuenced by Lo`eve (1997).
I use many other books for ﬁnding diﬀerent proofs, statements, exam-
ples, etc. For example, the approach of the product measure is taken
from Revuz (1994). We used notations from Kacimi (1999) to propose
a mode advanced way to make a comparison between the Lebesgue
and the Riemann integrals based on semi-continuous functions. The
readings of the seminal book of Halmos (1950) and the extremely rich
books of Bogachev (2007a) and Bogachev (2007b) allowed to provide
very useful examples and orientation. For example, the exercises on

(2007b).
But at the arrival, most of the results been stated as felt in classes by
the students and myself.

1. SETS, SETS OPERATIONS AND APPLICATIONS
DOC 00-01 : Sets operations - A summary.
NOTATION..
Ωis a reference set. Its elements are denoted by ω.
∅is the empty set.
A, B, C, .... are subsets of Ω(Capital normal letters).
(An)n≥0 , (Bn)n≥0, (Cn)n≥0 denote sequences of subsets of Ω.
P(Ω) is the power set of Ω: the collection of all subsets of Ω.
A, B, C, D, S, ..., denote collections of subsets of Ω, that is subsets of
P(Ω) (calligraphic font).
I - BASIC DEFINITIONS.
The operations on subsets of Ωare the following.
(00.00) Inclusion.
A set A is contained in a set B or is equal to B, denoted A ⊆B or
A ⊂B if and only if :
∀x ∈A, x ∈B.
A set A is strictly contained in a set B, denoted A ⊊B if and only if :

∀x ∈A, x ∈B

and

∃x ∈A, x /∈B

or, equivalently,
A ⊆B and A̸ = B.
(00.01) Complement of a subset A.
Ac = {x ∈Ω, x /∈A}
===== page 25 =====
1. SETS, SETS OPERATIONS AND APPLICATIONS
15
(00.02) Finite union.
(a) Union of two sets A and B :
A ∪B = {x ∈Ω, x ∈A or x ∈B}.
(b) Symmetry of the union operator.
A ∪B = B ∪A.
(c) Associativity of the union operator.
(A ∪B) ∪C = A ∪(B ∪C) = A ∪B ∪C.
(c) Union of a ﬁnite number k, (k ≥2) of sets
k[
n=1
An = {x ∈Ω, (∃n, 1 ≤n ≤k), x ∈An}.
(00.03) Finite intersection.
(a) Intersection of two sets A and B.
A ∩B = {x ∈Ω, x ∈A and x ∈B}.
(b) Symmetry of the intersection operator.
A ∩B = B ∩A.
(c) Associativity of the intersection operator.
(A ∩B) ∩C = A ∩(B ∩C) = A ∩B ∩C.
(c) Intersection of a ﬁnite number k (k ≥2) of sets
k\
n=1
An = {x ∈Ω, (∀n, 1 ≤n ≤k), x ∈An}.

1. SETS, SETS OPERATIONS AND APPLICATIONS
(00.04) Sum of subsets : If A and B are disjoint, that is A ∩B = ∅,
we denote
A ∪B = A + B.
(00.05) Diﬀerence A from B
B ⧹A = {x ∈Ω, x ∈B and x /∈A} = BAc.
(00.06) Symmetrical diﬀerence of A and B :
A ∆B = (B ⧹A) + (A ⧹B).

V - product spaces.
Let Ω1 and Ω2 be two non-empty sets. The product space Ω= Ω1 ×Ω2
is the set of all couples (ω1, ω2), where ω1 ∈Ω1 and ω2 ∈Ω2, that is
Ω= Ω1 × Ω2 = {(ω1, ω2), ω1 ∈Ω1 and ω2 ∈Ω2}.
A subset of the product space is a rectangle if it is the product of two
subsets of Ω1 and Ω2, that is
A × B with A ⊆Ω1, B ⊆Ω2
The rectangle A × B is deﬁned as
A × B = {(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω,
ω2 ∈A and ω2 ∈B}
(00.20) Not every subset of Ωis a rectangle (Unfortunately, taking all
subsets of a product space as rectangles is a very recurrent mistake).
Here is a counterexample.
Let Ω= Ω0 × Ω0 = Ω2
0 be the product of one space Ω0 by itself and
consider its diagonal
D = {(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω,
ω1 = ω2}.
Suppose that Ω0 has at least two elements. So D is not a rectangle.
Hint : To see that, suppose that D = A × B. Show that this is not
possible by considering two cases : (1) Both A and B are singletons,
(2) A or B has at least two elements.
(00.21) Properties of rectangles.
A × B = ∅if and only if A = ∅or B = ∅
(A × B) ∩(A′ × B′) = (A ∩A′) × (B ∩B′)
(A × B)c = Ac × B + A × Bc + Ac × Bc

Exercise 7. Let Ω= Ω1 ×Ω2 be a non-empty product deﬁned by
Ω= Ω1 × Ω2 = {(ω1, ω2), ω1 ∈Ω1 and ω2 ∈Ω2}.

Deﬁne a rectangle
A × B = {(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω, ω1 ∈A and ω2 ∈B},
where A ⊆Ω1, B ⊆Ω2.
(a) Show that the intersection of rectangles is a rectangle.
Solution : Let A × B and C × D be two rectangles. Let us form the
intersection :
(A × B) ∩(C × D)
=
{(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω, (ω1, ω2) ∈A × B and (ω1, ω2) ∈C × D}
=
{(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω, ω1 ∈A and ω2 ∈B and ω1 ∈C and ω2 ∈D}
=
{(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω, ω1 ∈A ∩C and ω2 ∈B ∩D}
A ∩C × B ∩D.
(b) Let A × B be a rectangle. Show that
(A × B)c = Ac × B + A × Bc + Ac × Bc.
Solution. We may partition Ωin the following way : for each (ω1, ω2) ∈
Ω, the four exclusive cases my occur : ω1 ∈A and ω2 ∈B or ω1 /∈A
and ω2 ∈B or ω1 ∈A and ω2 /∈B or ω1 /∈A and ω2 /∈B. This gives
Ω= A × B + Ac × B + A × Bc + Ac × Bc.
Then the complement of A × B is Ac × B + A × Bc + Ac × Bc.
(c) Show that not all subsets of are rectangles. Use the following ex-
ample.
Let Ω0 = Ω1 = Ω2 so that Ω= Ω0 × Ω0 = Ω2
0 is the product of one
space Ω0 by itself and consider its diagonal
D = {(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω,
ω1 = ω2}.
Suppose that Ω0 has at least two elements. Suppose that D = A × B.
Show that this is not possible by considering two cases : (1) Both A
and B are singletons, (2) A or B has at least two elements.
===== page 56 =====
46
1. SETS, SETS OPERATIONS AND APPLICATIONS
Solution. Suppose that ω1, ω2 be two elements of Ω0.
Suppose that
D = A × B.
(1) Suppose that A and B are singletons. Let A = {a} and B = {b}.
Then
A × B = {(a, b)}.
If D = A × B = {(a, b)}, then a = b and D = {(a, a)} is a singleton.
But, at least, D has at least the two elements (ω1, ω1) and (ω2, ω2).
This is impossible.
(2) Suppose that A has at least two elements ω1 and ω2. Let ω3 ∈B.
But D = A × B implies that (ω1, ω3) ∈D and (ω2, ω3) ∈D. This
implies that ω1 = ω2 = ω3. This is impossible since ω1̸ = ω2.

2. MEASURABLE SETS
DOC 01-01 : Measurable sets - A summary.
Speaking about measurable sets on Ωrequires ﬁrst one deﬁnes a ﬁeld
or a σ-algebra A of subsets on Ω. The pair (Ω, A) is called a measur-
able space.
I - σ-algebras.
(01.01) (Deﬁnition) A collection A of subsets of Ω, that is : A ⊂P(Ω),
is a ﬁeld or a σ-algebra of subsets of Ω, if and only if (it contains Ω
and ∅), and it is stable by all ﬁnite and countable sets operations.
NB. In this document and related ones, countable means ﬁnite or
inﬁnite countable.
(01.02) If A is σ-algebra of subsets of Ω, the couple (Ω, A) is said to
be a measurable space. The elements of A are called measurable sets.
(01.03) Warning : Never use measurability, without having in mind
the underlying σ-algebra we are working with.
(01.04) Criterion : To prove that A is σ-algebra of subsets of Ω, we
only need to check a few number of conditions. For example, we may
check.
(i) Ω∈A or ∅∈A.
(ii) A is stable under complementation : A ∈A =⇒Ac ∈A.
(iii) (A is stable under countable unions : {An, n ≥0} ⊂A =⇒
S
n≥0
An ∈A ) or (A is stable under countable intersections : {An, n ≥
0} ⊂A =⇒T
n≥0
An ∈A ).
(01.05) Examples of σ-algebras : (1) A = {∅, Ω}, (2) A = P(Ω), and
A = {A ⊂Ω, A is countable or Ac is countable }.
===== page 59 =====
2. MEASURABLE SETS
49
(01.06) In general, usual σ-algebras are not explicitly known as in
(01.05). A σ-algebra may be obtained by giving a speciﬁc sub-collection
C of it, in the following way : There exists a smallest σ-algebra (in the
sense of inclusion) that contains C. This σ-algebra is denoted σ(C) and
is called : the σ-algebra generated by C. In summary, we simultane-
ously have three points



(i) σ(C) is a σ-algebra,
(ii) C ⊂σ(C),
(iii) For any other σ-algebra D such that C ⊂D, we have : σ(C) ⊂D.
(01.07) Example : (1) The usual σ-algebra on R is generated by the
class I of all intervals of R.
For a topological space (E, T) where
T is class of open sets, the usual σ-algebra on B(T), called the Borel
σ-algebra, is generated by open sets. It is also generated by closed sets.
(01.08) Very important example : The product σ-algebra. Let (Ωi, Ai)
two measurable spaces and let Ω= Ω1 × Ω2 = {(ω1, ω2), ω1 ∈Ω1, ω2 ∈
Ω2} be the Cartesian product of Ω1 and Ω2. The class of cylinders (or
rectangles) is given by
S = {A1 × A2, A1 ∈A1, A2 ∈A2}.
The product σ-algebra on Ω= Ω1 × Ω2, denoted as
A1 ⊗A2, is
generated by S :
A1 ⊗A2 = σ(S).
(Ω1 × Ω2, A1 ⊗A2) is called the product measurable space.
===== page 60 =====
50
2. MEASURABLE SETS
II - Algebra.
Usually, the class C that generates our working σ-algebra, is an alge-
bra. What is an algebra of subsets of Ω?
(01.09) (Deﬁnition) A collection C of subsets of Ω, that is C ⊂P(Ω)
is an algebra of subsets of Ω, if and only if (it contains Ωand ∅), and
it is stable by all ﬁnite sets operations.
(01.10) Criterion : To prove that C is an algebra of subsets of Ω, we
only need to check a few number of conditions. For example, we may
check :
(i) Ω∈C or ∅∈C
(ii) C is stable under complementation : A ∈C =⇒Ac ∈A.
(iii) (C is stable under ﬁnite unions : {A, B} ⊂C =⇒A ∪B ∈C ) or
(C is stable under ﬁnite intersections : {A, B} ⊂C =⇒A ∩B ∈C)
(01.11) Examples : (1) C = {∅, Ω}, C = P(Ω), C = {A ⊂Ω, A is ﬁnite
or Ac is ﬁnite}.
(01.12) In general, usual algebras, are not explicitly known as in
(01.11). An algebra may be obtained by giving a speciﬁc sub-collection
S of it, in the following way : There exists a smallest algebra (in the
sens of inclusion) that contains S. This algebra is denoted a(S) and is
called : the algebra generated by S. In summary, we simultaneously
have three points :



(i) a(S) is an algebra,
(ii) S ⊂σ(S),
(iii) For any other algebra D such that S ⊂D, we have : σ(C) ⊂D
.
===== page 61 =====
2. MEASURABLE SETS
51
III - Semi-algebras.
Usually, the class S that generates our working
algebra, is a semi-
algebra. What is a semi-algebra of subsets of Ω.
(01.013) (Deﬁnition) A collection S of subsets of Ω, that is S ⊂P(Ω),
is a semi-algebra of subsets of Ω, if and only if :



Ω∈S
S is stable by ﬁnite intersections.
The complement of any element of S is a ﬁnite sum of elements of S .
NB : We take the elements of S as ﬁnite sums of elements of S with
one term. Besides, the empty belongs to S and each element of S is a
sum with the empty set.
(01.14) : The algebra generated by a semi-algebra S is the class of
all ﬁnite sums of its elements
a(S) = {A1 + A1 + ... + Ak, Ai ∈S, k ≥1}.
(01.15) Examples.
(a) The class I of all intervals of R is a semi-algebra (see (01.07)).
(b) The class I = {]a, b], −∞≤a ≤b ≤+∞} is a semi-algebra on R.
NB. On R, interpret ]a, +∞] ≡]a, +∞[.
(c) The class of cylinders in a product space (see (01.08)) is a semi-
algebra.
===== page 62 =====
52
2. MEASURABLE SETS
IV - Special subsets classes.
(01.16) Monotone class. A nonempty class M of subsets of Ωis
said to be monotone if and only if its contains the limits of its mono-
tone sequences.
That is, if (An)n≥0 is a non-decreasing sequence of
subsets in M, then ∪n≥0An is still in M, and as well if (An)n≥0 is a
non-increasing sequence of subsets in M, then ∩n≥0An is still in M.
(01.17) (Dynkin system or λ-system). A collection D of subsets of Ω.
is a Dynkin system if and only if
(i) Ω∈D,
(ii) For any (A, B) ∈D2 with A ⊂B, B\A ∈D,
(iii) For any sequence (An)n≥0) of pairwise disjoint elements of D,
P
n≥1 An ∈D.
(01.18) (π-system). A nonempty collection Π of subsets Ω. is a π-
system if and only if it is stable under ﬁnite intersection.
NB : These classes are used for demonstration purposes only.
For
example, the λ −µ method is base on them (See Exercise 11 in Doc
04-02, Chapter 5, page 233).

Exercise 1. Show that the class I of all intervals of R is a semi-algebra.
Solution.
Check the three points.
(a) Ω= R is an interval, so R ∈I.
(b) The intersection of two intervals of R is an interval of R. So I is
stable under ﬁnite intersections. You should remind this formula :

] max(x1, y1), min(x2, y2)[.

type.

Exercise 2. Show that the class of rectangles S is a semi-algebra.

Solution.
Let (Ωi, Ai) two measurable spaces and let Ω= Ω1×Ω2 = {(ω1, ω2), ω1 ∈
Ω1, ω2 ∈Ω2} be the Cartesian product of Ω1 and Ω2. The class of cylin-

S = {A1 × A2, A1 ∈A1, A2 ∈A2}.
We have to show that S is a semi-algebra.Check the three conditions.
(a) It is obvious that Ω= Ω1 × Ω2 ∈S.

(b) Let A = A1×A2 ∈S,with A1 ∈A1, A2 ∈A2 and B = B1×B2 ∈S,

A ∩B
=

=
{(ω1, ω2) ∈Ω, (ω1 ∈A1 and × ω1 ∈B1) and (ω2 ∈A2 and ω2 ∈B2)}

(A1 ∩B1) × (A2 ∩B2).

Exercise 3. Le Ωbe a nonempty set and set
A = {A ⊂Ω,
A is finite orAc is finite}.
(a) Show that A is an algebra.
(b) Show that A is a σ-algebra if Ωis ﬁnite.
(c) If Ωis inﬁnite, consider an inﬁnite subset C = {ω0, ω1, ω2, ..} where
the ωi are dsijoint and set An = {ω2n}, n ≥0. Show that the An are
in A and their union is not.
(d) Conclude that A is a σ-algebra if and only if Ωis ﬁnite.
Solution.
(a) Let us check that A is an algebra.
===== page 71 =====
2. MEASURABLE SETS
61
(i) Ωbelongs to A since its complement is empty and then ﬁnite.
(ii) The roles of A and Ac are symmetrical in the deﬁnition of A. So A
belongs to A whenever Ac belongs to A and vice-verse.
(iii) Let us show that A is stable under ﬁnite unions. Let A and B be
two elements of A. we have two case. Either A and B are ﬁnite sets
and then A∪B is ﬁnite and hence A∪B belongs to A. Or one of them
is not ﬁnite. Suppose that A is not ﬁnite. Since A is in A and is not
ﬁnite, this implies that Ac is ﬁnite (otherwise A would not be in A).
Then, we have
(A ∪B)c = Ac ∩Bc ⊂Ac.
Thus (A ∪B)c is ﬁnite as part of a ﬁnite set. Hence A ∪B ∈A. Then
in all possibles cases, A ∪B belongs to A whenever A and B are in A.
(b) Since A is already an algebra, if suﬃces to show that is is stable
under countable unions or intersections. But if Ωis a ﬁnite set, the
collection of subsets is also ﬁnite and so, a countable union of elements
of A is merely a ﬁnite union of element of A and then remains in A.
Thus, it is clear that A is an sigma-algebra if Ωis ﬁnite.
(c) Clearly the sets An are ﬁnite and then, they belong to A. But their
union
A = ∪n≥0An = {ω0, ω2, ...}
is neither ﬁnite nor co-ﬁnite since
{ω1, ω3, ...} ⊂Ac.
(d) The conclusion is : A is a sigma-algebra if and only if Ωis ﬁnite.

Exercise 6. Let X be an application from Ω1 to Ω2 and suppose that

etc.

(c) Let ∀(An)n≥0 ⊂AX. Then for each n ≥1, there exists Bn ∈A2 such

(1) Clearly X is measurable from (Ω1, AX) to (Ω2, A2).

(3) Final conclusion : AX is the smallest sigma-algebra on Ω1 such that
X : Ω1 7−→(Ω2, A2) is measurable.

Exercise 10. Let Ωbe a nonempty set and let D a collection of subsets
of Ω. Show that
σ(D) = σ(a(D)).
Solution.
(a) Direct inclusion. a(D) contains D. So the smallest sigma-algebra
including D, that is σ(a(D)), contains D. Since σ(a(D)) is a sigma-
algebra including D, it contains the smallest sigma-algebra including
D, that is : σ(D) ⊂σ(a(D)).
(b) Indirect inclusion : σ(D) is an algebra including D (do not forget
that a sigma-algebra is also an algebra), so its contains the small-
est algebra including a(D), that is :
a(D) ⊂σ(D). Now σ(D) is
a sigma-algebra including a(D), then it contains σ(a(D)), that is :
σ(a(D)) ⊂σ(D).
Putting together the two points leads to the desired conclusion.

Abstract Monotone and Dynkin classes are important tools for theo-
retical demonstrations in Measure Theory. It is important to be famil-
iar with them. Their importance reside in Exercises 3 and 4 below.
You will need these basic and easy facts. A σ-algebra is an algebra, is
a Dynkin system and is a monotone class. An algebra is a π-system.
Exercise 1. Let C be an algebra and a monotone class. Show that C
is a sigma-algebra.
Exercise 2. Let D be a π-class and a Dynkin system. Show that D is
a sigma-algebra.
Exercise 3. Let C be an algebra of subsets of Ω. Prove that σ(C) =
m(C).
Proposed solution (in Lo`eve)
Denote M = m(C)
Deﬁne for any subset A of Ω
MA = {B ∈M, ABc ∈M, AcB ∈M, AB ∈M}.
A1. Show that for any A ∈M, MA is a monotone class containing A.
A2.(i) Show that for any A ∈C, C ⊂MA. Deduce from this, that
MA = M.
A2.(ii) Let B ∈M. Use the symmetry of the roles of A and B in the
deﬁnition B ∈MA and A2.(i) to prove that A ∈MB for any A ⊂C,
and then C ⊂MB. Conclude that MB = M.
===== page 88 =====
78
2. MEASURABLE SETS
A3 Conclude that for any (A, B) ∈M2,
AB ∈M, ABc ∈M, AcB ∈M
and prove from this, M is a σ-algebra.
A4. Conclude.
Exercise 4.
Let G be a π-system of subsets of Ω.
Show that :
σ(G) = d(G).
Proposed solution.

: Denote D as the Dynkin system generated by G. Put
DA = {B ∈D, AB ∈D}.

B2. Prove that for any A ∈G, DA is a Dynkin system including G.
Conclude that : DA = D.
B3.

B ∈DA, show that, for any B ∈D, G ⊂DB and then DB = D.
B4. Show then that D is stable by intersection and then is a σ-algebra.
B5. Conclude.

set An = {ω2n}, n ≥0. Show that the An are in A and their union is
not.
(d) Conclude that A is a σ-algebra if and only if Ωis ﬁnite.
Solution.
(a) Let us check that A is an algebra.
(i) Ωbelongs to A since its complement is empty and then ﬁnite.
(ii) The roles of A and Ac are symmetrical in the deﬁnition of A. So A
belongs to A whenever Ac belongs to A and vice-verse.
(iii) Let us show that A is stable under ﬁnite unions. Let A and B be
two elements of A. we have two case. Either A and B are ﬁnite sets
and then A∪B is ﬁnite and hence A∪B belongs to A. Or one of them
is not ﬁnite. Suppose that A is not ﬁnite. Since A is in A and is not
ﬁnite, this implies that Ac is ﬁnite (otherwise A would not be in A).
Then
(A ∪B)c = Ac ∩Bc ⊂Ac.
Thus (A ∪B)c is ﬁnite as part of a ﬁnite set. Hence A ∪B ∈A. Then
in all possibles cases, A ∪B belongs to A whenever A and B are in A.
===== page 100 =====
90
2. MEASURABLE SETS
(b) Since A is already an algebra, if suﬃces to show that is is stable
under countable unions or intersections. But if Ωis a ﬁnite set, the
collection of subsets is also ﬁnite and so, a countable union of elements
of A is merely a ﬁnite union of element of A and then remains in A.
Thus, it is clear that A is an sigma-algebra if Ωis ﬁnite.
(c) Clearly the sets An are ﬁnite and then, they belong to A. But their
union
A = ∪n≥0An = {ω0, ω2, ...}
is neither ﬁnite nor co-ﬁnite since
{ω1, ω3, ...} ⊂Ac.
(d) The conclusion is : A is a sigma-algebra if and only if Ωis ﬁnite.
Exercise 2.
Let Ω= {ω1, ..., ω2n} , where n is ﬁnite ﬁxed integer such that n ≥2,
be a space of even cardinality. Let A the collection of all subsets of Ω
having an even cardinality.
(a) Show that A is a Dynkin system.
(b) Use A = {ω1, ω2}and B = {ω1, ω3}. to show that A is not stable
by ﬁnite intersection?
(c) Make a conclusion.
Solution.
It is important here to remark that n is ﬁnite. So any union of sub-
sets of Ωis a ﬁnite union. Now let us show that A is a Dynkin system.
(i) Ω∈A ? We have to check that Ωhas an even cardinality. This is
true since the cardinality of Ωis 2n, and then even.
===== page 101 =====
2. MEASURABLE SETS
91
(ii) Let A and B in A with A ⊂B. We have to check that B\A is in
A. But A ∈A and B ∈A mean that their cardinals #(A) and #(B)
are both even. Also, since A ⊂B holds, we have
#(B\A) = #(B) −#(A)
Since #(B\A) is diﬀerence of even integers, it is also even.
Thus
(B\A) ∈A.
(iii) Let (An)n≥0 a pairwise disjoint elements of A. We have to prove
that A = P
n An ∈A. But as said earlier, since Ωis ﬁnite, any union
reduces to a ﬁnite union. So P

1≤k≤p Bk,
where Bk ∈A and p is ﬁnite. Then
#(A) =
X
1≤k≤p
#(Bk)
and #(A) is even as a ﬁnite sum of even integers. Thus A = P
n An ∈
A.
We may conclude that A is a Dynkin system.
(b) : Now, since Ωhas at least four elements, we see that by the no-
tation, {ω1, ω2, ω3} ⊂Ω. Clearly A = {ω1, ω2}and B = {ω1, ω3} have
even cardinality and then, are in . But their intersection A∩B = {ω1}
has an odd cardinality (1) and then does not lie in A.
(c) : Conclusion : A Dyskin system is not necessarily stable by ﬁnite
intersection, and then, it is not necessarily an algebra.
Remark : You will see in the homework 01 that a Dynkyn system which
is also a π-system, is an algebra and then a sigma-algebra.

3. MEASURABLE APPLICATIONS
DOC 02-01 - Measurable Applications - A sum-
mary.
Abstract. Measurable applications are applications preserving mea-
surability of sets by means of inverse images. Before you continue, you
are asked to go back to Exercise 13 in Doc 01-02 (page 55) and to its
solutions in Doc 01.03 in Chapter 2 (page 72), to revise the properties
of inverse images, in particular to get acquainted with the fact that the
inverse image preserves all sets operations.
(02.01) Deﬁnition of measurable applications.
An application X from the measurable space (Ω1, A1) to the measur-
able space (Ω2, A2), is measurable, with respect to the two σ-algebras
A1 and A2, and we say that X is (A1-A2)-measurable, if and only if
(M)
∀(B ∈A2), X−1(B) ∈A1,
meaning : the reciprocal image of a measurable subset of Ω2 is a mea-
surable subset of Ω1.
NB. In measure theory, It is usual to use this notation : X−1(B) =
(X ∈B). Never forget the deﬁnition of this set : (X ∈B) = {ω ∈
Ω1, X(ω) ∈B} ⊂Ω1
Condition (M) is equivalent to :
(MS)
AX = {(X ∈B), B ∈A2} ⊂A1.
(02.02) Weakening the deﬁnition.
Fortunately, in many cases, A2 is generated by a small class, say C,
that is A2 = σ(C). In these cases, the application X is measurable if
and only if
(WMC)
∀(B ∈C), X−1(B) ∈A1.
Catch this : the condition (WMC) is called a weak measurability
condition. It is very useful in many situations. Immediate examples
are provided in Points (02.04) and (02.05) below.
===== page 107 =====
3. MEASURABLE APPLICATIONS
97
(02.03). Measurability of composition of applications.
Let X be a measurable application from (Ω1, A1) to (Ω2, A2), and let
Y be a measurable application form (Ω2, A2) to (Ω3, A3).
Then the composition of applications (function of function) Z = Y (X) =
Y X deﬁned from (Ω1, A1) to (Ω3, A3) by
Ω1 ∋ω֒ →Z(ω) = Y (X(ω))
is measurable.
(Reminder) : In terms of inverse images, we have
Y (X)−1(C) = X−1(Y −1(C)).
(02.04) Simple application to continuous functions.
Let X be an application from the topological (E1, T1) to the topological
space (E2, T2), where T1 and T2 are the classes of open sets of the two
topological spaces. By deﬁnition, X is continuous if and only if
(C)
∀(B ∈T2), X−1(B) ∈T1,
.
Now let B(E1) = σ(T1) and B(E2) = σ(T2) be the corresponding Borel
σ-algebras on E1 and E2 respectively. Then (C) implies
(0.1)
∀(B ∈T2), X−1(B) ∈B(E1),
since T1 ⊂B(E1). By (WMC), X is measurable with respect to the
Borel σ-algebra.
Conclusion : A continuous application is measurable with respect to
the Borel ﬁelds. As a consequence, the class of measurable functions is
broader than the class of continuous functions.

uct σ-algebra.

DOC 02-02 : What you cannot ignore on Measura-

Abstract. Measurable applications are applications preserving mea-

Before you continue,

operations.

(Ω1, A1) onto the measurable space (Ω2, A2), is measurable, with re-
spect to (A1), A2)), if
(M)
∀(B ∈A2), X−1(B) ∈A1,

X−1(B) = (X ∈B)
(02.09) : Criterion. Fortunately, in many cases, A2 is generated by
a small class, say C, that is A2 = σ(C). In these cases, the application

(M)
∀(B ∈C), X−1(B) ∈A1,

(02.13) : Operations in E, the class of all elementary functions.
If f and g are elementary functions, and c is a real number, then f +g,
fg, cf, max(f, g) and min(f, g) are elementary functions. If g does not
take the null value, then f/g is an elementary function.

(02.16) : Operations on real-valued measurable functions.
If f and g are measurable functions, and c is a real number, then f +g,
fg, cf, max(f, g) and min(f, g) are measurable whenever theses func-
tions are well-deﬁned. If g does not take the null value, then f/g is
measurable.

And so forth.

Exercise 4. (Weak measurability condition) Let X be an application
from the measurable (Ω1, A1) to the measurable space (Ω2, A2) and
suppose that A2 is generated by a nonempty class C of subsets
Ω2,
that is A2 = σ(C). Suppose that
(WMC)
∀(B ∈C), X−1(B) ∈A1,
(a) Show that the following class of subsets of Ω2
A2,c = {B ∈A2, X−(B) ∈A1}.
satisﬁes the three properties :
(i) A2,c is a σ-algebra.
(ii) A2,c is included in A2 : A2,c ⊂A2.
(iii) A2,c includes C : C ⊂A2,c.
(b) Deduce from this that A2,c = A2 and then
(WMC)
∀(B ∈A1), X−1(B) ∈A1,
===== page 123 =====
3. MEASURABLE APPLICATIONS
113
that is, X is measurable.
SOLUTIONS.
Question (a).
Point (i). Use the properties of the inverse images of sets (Doc 01-02,
Exercise 13, solutions in Doc 01-03). We are going to prove that A2,c
is a σ-algebra on Ω2. Let us check the following points.
(1) Ω2 ∈A2,c? Yes since X−1(Ω2) = Ω1 ∈A1.
(2) Let B ∈A2,c. Do we have Bc ∈A2,c? Yes since X−1(B) ∈A1 and
X−1(Bc) = (X−1(B))c . Then X−1(Bc) ∈A1 and thus, B ∈A2,c.
(3) Let (Bn)n≥1 ⊂A2,c. Do we have ∪n≥1Bn ∈A2,c? Yes since
X−1(∪n≥1Bn) = ∪n≥1X−1(Bn) ∈A1
since, by assumption, each X−1(Bn) ∈A1. Thus ∪n≥1Bn ∈A2,c.
Point (ii). We have A2,c ⊂A2 by construction.
Point (iii). We have C ⊂A2,c by the assumption (WMC).
Question (b). Since A2,c is a σ-algebra including C then it includes
σ(C) = A2. Now, since A2,c is in A2 by construction, we get A2,c = A2.
We get
(M)
∀(B ∈A2), X−1(B) ∈A1.
This means that X is measurable. So Condition (WMC) implies (M).
Reversely, (M) obviously implies (WMC). We conclude that X is mesaurable
if and only if (WMC) holds.

4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
DOC 03-01 : Borel sets Rd and Rd-valued Measur-
able functions.
Part I - The space R.
(03.01) The usual σ-algebra on R, denoted B(R), is generated by the
whole class of intervals of R denoted I. And we know that I is a semi-
algebra. But we have more detailed things.
(03.02) Actually B(R) is generated by any class of one particular type
of intervals.
Deﬁne :
I(1) = {] −∞, a[, a ∈R} , I(2) = {] −∞, a],
a ∈R};
I(3) = {] −∞, a], a ∈R} , I(4) = {] −∞, a],
a ∈R}
;
I(5) = {]a, +∞[, a ∈R}, I(6) = {[a, +∞[,
a ∈R}
I(7) = {[a, +∞[, a ∈R} , I(8) = {]a, +∞[,
a ∈R};
I(9) = {]a, b], a, b ∈R} , I(10) = {]a, b],
(a, b) ∈R
2};
I(11) = {]a, b[, a, b ∈R} , I(12) = {]a, b[, (a, b) ∈R
2};
I(13) = {[a, b[, a, b ∈R} , I(14) = {[a, b[, (a, b) ∈R
2};
I(15) = {[a, b], a, b ∈R} , I(16) = {[a, b], (a, b) ∈R
2}.
===== page 133 =====
4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
123
Important Fact. Any of these classes generates B(R).
(03.03) Borel application. A real-valued and measurable application
is called a Borel function. By using the criterion of measurability for
the diﬀerent generation classes in Point 03.02, we have a number of
propositions, each of them characterizing measurability of functions
X : (Ω, A) →(R, B(R)).
Before we give some of these criteria, we introduce the notation :
For B ⊂R, X−1(B) = {ω ∈Ω, X(ω) ∈B} ≡(X ∈B).
For example, for a, b ∈R,
(X < a) = X−1([−∞, a[),
(X ≤a) = X−1([−∞, a]),
(a < X ≤b) = X−1(]a, b]),
(a < X < b) = X−1(]a, b[),
etc.
So, the real-valued application X is measurable if and only if
(C1) : For all a ∈R, (X < a) ∈A,
if and only if
(C1) : For all a ∈R, (X ≤a) ∈A,
if and only if
(C1) : For all a ∈R, (X > a) ∈A,
if and only if
(C1) : For all a ∈R, (X ≥a) ∈A,
etc. (See Solutions of Exercise 2 in DOC 03-04 below, 142, in Doc
03-07, page 159, for a more detailed list of criteria).
Part II - Working in the extended real line R.

Part II (a) - The space R.
(03.04) We usually work on the extended real line
R = [−∞, +∞] = R ∪{−∞, +∞}.
The extended real line is obtained by adjoining −∞and +∞to R. To
deﬁne the sigma-algebra on R, we consider the elements of B∞(R) and
their unions with {+∞}, {−∞} and with {−∞, +∞}, that is
B∞(R) = {A, A ∪{+∞}, A ∪{+∞}, A ∪{−∞, +∞}}.
We have these remarkable points.
(03.05) B∞(R) this is smallest sigma-algebra for which the Borel sets
of
R, {−∞}, {+∞}, and {−∞, +∞} are measurable. You will not
have any problem to show that each of the following classes of subsets
also generates B∞(R).
(03.06) - Generation of B∞(R). The same classes I(1), ..., I(14) given
above generate B∞(R).
Part II (b) General Borel Functions.

(03.07) For any real-valued function, possibly taking inﬁnite values,
X : (Ω, A) →(R, B∞(R))
is measurable if and only if (C1) holds, that is,
For all a ∈R, (X < a) ∈A,
if and only if (C2) holds, that is,
For all a ∈R, (X ≤a) ∈A,
if and only if (C3) holds, that is,

limit, and so, it is measurable.
Rule : Limits of measurable applications are measurable.
Part IV. Examples of measurable sets of R.
It is not easy to ﬁnd non-measurable sets on by simple means. Exam-
ples of non measurable sets come after very complicated mathematics.
Any singleton {a}, a ∈R, is measurable since {a} = [a, a] is an interval.
Finite subsets of R are measurable as ﬁnite unions of singletons.
Countable subsets R are measurable as countable unions of singletons.
The set of rationals Q is measurable since it is countable. The set of
irrationals is also measurable as the complement of Q.
etc.

(d) Examples of Measurable sets in R
k.
As for the special class k = 1, non-measurable sets are not easy to
get in R
k. Almost all the classical subsets we know, are measurable.
This is not a reason to get for granted that every subset of R
k is mea-
surable in R
k. The following list of measurable types of subsets of R
k
is given only for illustration.
(d1) Intervals in R
k are measurable by construction.
(d2) Singletons in R
k are measurable as since they are intervals
(a1, ..., ak) = [a1, a1] × ... × [ak, ak].
(d3) Countable sets are measurable.
(d4) Qk is measurable as a product of measurable sets in Q. Jk is
measurable for the same reason (Q is the set of rational numbers, J is
the set if irrational numbers in R).
===== page 139 =====
4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
129
(d5) Hyperplanes are measurable. Hyperplanes are linear subspaces
of Rk of dimension k −1. Any hyperplane H may be deﬁned by an
equation like : there exist real numbers a1, a2, .., ak such that a1 × a2 ×
... × ak̸ = 0 and a real number b such that
H = {(x1, ...., xk) ∈Rk, a1x1 + .... + akxk + b = 0}.
Since the application h(x1, ...., xk) = a1x1 + .... + akxk + b is contin-
uous from Rk to R is continuous and the measurable. And we have
H = h−1({0}). Hence H is measurable as the inverse image of the
measurable set {0} in R.
(d6) Aﬃne subspaces in Rk are measurable.
To see this, proceed as follows. An aﬃne subspace of Rk is of the form
a + H = {a + x, x ∈H}, where b ∈Rk and H is a linear subspace of
Rk. By b + H is the inverse image of the translation t−b(x) = x −b,
which is continuous and then measurable. It is then clear that b + H
is measurable whenever H is. But the only linear subspace of Rk of
dimension k is Rk, the only linear subspace of Rk of dimension 0 is {0}
and ﬁnally, any linear subspace of Rk of dimension s with 0 < s < k is
intersection of k −s + 1 hyperplanes. Conclusion : any aﬃne subspace
of Rk is measurable.
(d7) Rectangles, cubes, hyper-cubes, squares, etc. are measurable as
intervals.
(d8) Parallelograms, parallelepiped, cylinders, etc. are measurable.
(d8.1) We may use the equations of all these geometric objects and use
their continuity of the functions involved (in the equations) to express
these objects as inverse images of measurable sets.
(d8.2) We may also the topological concepts of closure, boundary and
interior to establish measurability. You will learn more about this with
the course of topology.
(d6) etc..
(e) Non-measurable sets.

03.18 : Deﬁnition of an elementary function.

(03.19) Be careful : A simple function may have several expressions.
For example, we get another expression if we split one of the Ai.
(03.20) There exists a unique expression for an elementary function
of the form (0.7) with distinct values of αi, 1 ≤i ≤k.
(00.21) The class E of simple functions is a Riesz space, and is an
algebra of functions.

DOC 03-03 : Measurable applications - A summary.
I - General introduction.
1 : Deﬁnition. An application X from the measurable space (Ω1, A1)
onto the measurable space (Ω2, A2), is measurable, with respect to
(A1), A2)), if
(M)
∀(B ∈A2), X−1(B) ∈A1,
It is usual in measure theory to use the following notation :
X−1(B) = (X ∈B).
2 : Criterion. Fortunately, in many cases, A2 is generated by a small
class, say C, that is A2 = σ(C). In these cases, the application X is
measurable if
(M)
∀(B ∈C), X−1(B) ∈A1.
3 : Composition of measurable applications. Let X be a mea-
surable application from (Ω1, A1) onto (Ω2, A2), and let Y be a mea-
surable application form (Ω2, A2) onto (Ω3, A3). Then the composite
function (function of function) Z = Y (X) = Y X deﬁned from (Ω1, A1)
to (Ω3, A3) by
Ω1 ∋ω ⇒Z(ω) = Y (X(ω))
is measurable.

Exercise 2. (Algebra on the space of elementary functions E).
===== page 158 =====
148
4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
Let X, Y and Z be simple functions, a and b be real scalars.
By using Question (c) of Exercise 1, show the following properties :
(a1) aX + bY ∈E.
(a2) XY ∈E.
(a3) If Y is everywhere diﬀerent of zero, then X/Y ∈E.
(a5) (X ≤Y ) ⇒(X+Z ≤Y +Z). If a ≥0, then X ≤Y ⇒aX ≤bY .
(a6) max(A, X) ∈E, min(X, Y ) ∈E.
Terminology : (You may skip the remarks below).
(A) Needless to prove, the product of ﬁnite functions and the sum of
ﬁnite functions are commutative and associative. The product of ﬁnite
functions is distributive with the sum of ﬁnite functions. Taking into
account these facts, we state the following facts.
By (a1), (E, +, ·) is a linear space (where · stands for the external mul-
tiplication by real scalars).
By (a1) and (a2), and the remarks (A), (E, +, ×) is a ring with unit
1Ω(where × stands for the product between two functions), and is an
algebra of functions.
By (a6), (E, ≤) is a lattice space, meaning closed under ﬁnite maximum
and ﬁnite minimum.
By (a1), (a5) and (a6), (E, +, ·, ≤) is a Riesz space, that is a lattice
(a6) vector space (a1) such that the order is compatible with the linear
structure.

Question (a). It is easy to check that B∞(R) is a σ-algebra. By tak-
ing A = ∅, we also see that {+∞} and {−∞} are measurable and next
{−∞, +∞} is measurable. Finally, any σ-algebra including B(R) and
for which {−∞} and {+∞} are mea0surable includes B∞(R). Then
B∞(R) is the smallest σ-algebra for which the Borel sets of
R, and
{−∞}, {+∞} are measurable.

Exercise 2. (Algebra on the space of elementary functions E).
Let X, Y and Z be simple functions, a and b be real scalars.
By using Question (c) of Exercise 1, show the following properies :
(a1) aX + bY ∈E.
(a2) XY ∈E.
(a3) If Y is everywhere diﬀerent of zero, then X/Y ∈E.
(a5) (X ≤Y ) ⇒(X+Z ≤Y +Z). If a ≥0, then X ≤Y ⇒aX ≤bY .
(a6) max(A, X) ∈E, min(X, Y ) ∈E.
Terminology : (You may skip the remarks below).
(A) Needless to prove, the product of ﬁnite functions and the sum of
ﬁnite functions are commutative and associative. The product of ﬁnite
functions is distributive with the sum of ﬁnite functions. Taking into
account these facts, we state the following facts.
By (a1), (E, +, ·) is a linear space (where · stands for the external mul-
tiplication by real scalars).
By (a1) and (a2), and the remarks (A), (E, +, ×) is a ring with unit
1Ω(where × stands for the product between two functions), and is an
algebra of functions.
===== page 187 =====
4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
177
By (a6), (E, ≤) is a lattice space, meaning closed under ﬁnite maximum
and ﬁnite minimum.
By (a1), (a5) and (a6), (E, +, ·, ≤) is a Riesz space, that is a lattice
(a6) vector space (a1) such that the order is compatible with the linear
structure.

Exercise 1. Le f
be a non-decreasing function from R to R. Show
that the set of discontinuity points is at most countable.
Proceed to this way. Let D(n) the number discontinuity points of f
in ] −n, n[. Recall that a real number x is discontinuity point of a
non-decreasing function f if and only if
f(x+) −f(x−) > 0
where f(x−) is the left-hand limit of f at x and f(x+) is the right-hand
limit. In that case f(x+) −f(x−) is the discontinuity jump. Next let
Dk(n) = {x ∈D(n), f(x+) −f(x−) > 1/k}.
Pick x1, ..., xm from Dk(n) and justify the inequality
X
1≤i≤m
f(xi+) −f(xi−) ≤f(n) −f(−n).
Deduce from this that
m ≤k × (f(n) −f(−n)),
and then Dk(n) is ﬁnite.
Conclude.
Solution. Since f is non-decreasing, x is a discontinuity point of f if
and only if the discontinuity jump f(x+) −f(x−) is positive. Denote
by D the set of all discontinuity points of f, and for any k ≥1, denote
by Dk the set of discontinuity points such that f(x+) −f(x−) > 1/k
and by Dk,n the set of discontinuity points in the interval ] −n, n[ such
that f(x+) −f(x−) > 1/k. We are going to show that Dk,n is ﬁnite.
Let us suppose we can ﬁnd m points x1, ..., xm in Dk,n. Since f is
non-decreasing, we may see that the sum of the discontinuity jumps is
less than f(n) −f(−n). [ You may make a simple drawing for m = 3
and project the jumps to the y-axis to see this easily]. So
===== page 198 =====
188
4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
X
1≤j≤m
f(x+) −f(x−) ≤f(n) −f(−n).
Since each of these jumps exceeds 1/k, we have
X
1≤j≤m
(1/k) ≤
X
1≤j≤m
f(x+) −f(x−) ≤F(n) −F(−n).
m/k ≤f(n) −f(−n),
That is
m ≤k(f(n) −f(−n))
We conclude by saying that we cannot have more that [k(f(n)−f(−n))]
points in Dk,n, so Dk,n is ﬁnite. Since
D = ∪n≥1 ∪k≥1 D(k, n),
we see that D is countable. The solution is complete.

Exercise 3. Let X be an application from from R to R such that at
each t ∈R the left limit f(x−) exists (resp. at each t ∈R the right-
hand limit f(x+) exists.
(a) Use know facts on the left continuity of t 7→X(t−) and the left-
continuity of (x 7→X(t+))) to show that X is measurable with help of
Exercise 2.
(b) Suppose that at each t ∈R the left limit f(x−) exists (resp. at
each t ∈R the right-hand limit f(x+) exists). Show that the function
t 7→X(t−) is left-continuous (resp. (x 7→X(t+)) is right-continuous).
[You may skip this question and consider it as a topology exercise. But
===== page 201 =====
4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
191
you are recommended to read the proof].
Solution.
Question (a).
If for each t ∈R, the right-hand limit X(t+) ex-
ists, then by results of real analysis, the function t 7→X(t+) is right-
continuous. Hence, by Exercise 1, it is measurable.
As well, if for each t ∈R, the left-hand limit X(t−) exists, then by re-
sults of real analysis, the function t 7→X(t−) is left-continuous. Hence,
by Exercise 1, it is measurable.
Question (b). Let us prove the above mentioned properties of real
analysis. We give the solution only for the right-continuity case.
Assume that the left-limit X(t+) exists at each t ∈R. For any ﬁxed t,
we have
(∀ǫ > 0), (∃h0 > 0) : 0 < h < h0 ⇒, X(t+h)−ǫ ≤X(t+) ≤X(t+h)+ǫ. (LM)
Now, set Y (t) = X(t+). We have to prove that Y is right-continuous.
Fix t and ǫ > 0. From (LM), we have
(∀0 < h < h0/2, ∀0 < ℓ< h0/2), X(t+h+ℓ)−ǫ ≤X(t+) ≤X(t+h+ℓ)+ǫ. (RC1)
Then let ℓ↓0, we get
(∀0 < h < h0/2, X((t + h)+) −ǫ ≤X(t+) ≤X((t + h)+) + ǫ. (RC1)
Through (RC1), we have proved this : For any ǫ > 0, there exists
h1 = h0/2 such that
(∀0 < h < h1, Y (t + h) −ǫ ≤Y (t) ≤Y (t + h) + ǫ. (RC2)
The right-continuity of Y (t) = X(t+) is thus proved.
To treat the case where X has left-limits, do similarly by taking Z(t) =
X(t−). Be wise!

Exercise 5. Use Exercises 1 and 4 to show that any monotone func-
tion f : R →R is measurable.
Solution.
Let f : R →R be monotone.
Suppose that it is non-
decreasing. From Exercise 1, the set D of discontinuity points of f is
===== page 203 =====
4. MEASURABILITY IS USUAL SPACES
193
at most countable. Surely f has a left and right limit at each point
t ∈R. Indeed, ﬁx t ∈R. Since f is non-decreasing, the set
At = {F(x), x < t}
is bounded above by f(t). So by the Archimedean property of R, this
set has a ﬁnite supremum
α = sup At.
S Let us show that α is the left limit f(t−) of f at t. First, we have :
for any h > 0
f(t −h) ≤f(t).
S As h ↓0, f(t −h) increases in the large sense. Then f(t −h) has a
limit f(t−) which is still less than f(t). Since f is non-decreasing,the
increasing limit as h ↓0 is exactly the limit as h →0, which is equal
to f(t−).
Conversely, by the deﬁnition of the supremum, for any εn = 1/n, n ≥1,
there exists one element At, denoted f(yn) [ with xn < t ] that exceeds
α −εn that is
f(yn) ≥α −εn.
S Now either t −εn ≤yn < t and we put xn = yn oryn < t −εn and we
take xn = t −εn and the non-decreasingness of f, we have

f(xn) ≥α −εn
t −εn ≤xn < t.
Thus xn →t by left, we get by letting n →+∞.
f(t−) ≥α.
We conclude that
f(t−) = sup At.
A non-decreasing function has left-hand limits everywhere and has at
most number of discontinuity points. It is measurable.
If f is non-increasing, we apply the result we found to −f which is
non-decreasing. The function −f is measurable, so is f.

for each t ∈E,

Solution.

semi-continuous. Then it is measurable.

any real number c, the set F = (X ≤c) is closed.
X : (E, d) →R is lower-continuous at t means : for any
(∀0 < ǫ < 0)(∃η > 0), d(s, t) < η ⇒X(s) ≤X(t) + ǫ. (LSC)

s ∈Bd(t, η)

X is measurable.

5. MEASURES
DOC 04-01 : Measures - A summary.
Global picture :
Measures are modern and formal generalizations
of intuitive measures of lengths (in R), of areas (in R2), of volumes in
R3, etc.
Deﬁnition. Let m be an application from a class F of subsets of Ω
to R. The application m is said to be proper if and only if it exists an
element A of F such that m(A) is ﬁnite, that is |m(A)| < +∞.
Warning. In all this book, measures are meant as non-negative mea-
sures. Diﬀerences of non-negative measures are called signed measures.
I - DEFINITIONS.
04.01a (Deﬁnition) Let A be a σ-algebra of subsets of Ω. An applica-
tion m from A to R+ is a called a measure if and only if :
(i) m(∅)=0.
(ii) For all countable family (An)(n≥I), I ⊂N , of pairwise disjoint
elements of A, the σ-additivity formula holds
m(
X
n∈I
An) =
X
n∈I
m(An).
04.01b (A second deﬁnition). Alternatively, the application m from
A to R+ is a called a measure if and only if it is proper and σ-additivity.
04.02 Finite additivity. An application m from an algebra C to R+
is a said to be ﬁnitely additive if and only if for any ﬁnite sequence
(An)(0≤n≤k) of k pairwise disjoint elements of C, with k ≥1 and ﬁxed,
we have
m(
X
0≤n≤k
An) =
X
0≤n≤k
m(An).
Remark. Finite additivity is a special case of σ-additivity, where in
(ii), the set I is ﬁnite. Then, σ-additivity includes ﬁnite additivity.

04.03a σ-sub-additivity. An application m from A to R+ is a said to
be σ-sub-additive if and only if for all sequence (An)(n≥0) of elements
of A, we have
m(

X

m(An).
04.03b As well, an application m from an algebra A to R+ is a said to
be ﬁnitely sub-additive, or simply sub-additive, if and only if for any
ﬁnite sequence (An)(0≤n≤k) k elements of C, with k ≥1 and ﬁxed, we
have
m(

X
0≤n≤k
m(An).
04.04 If m is a measure on A, we say that (Ω, A, m) is a measure space.

5. MEASURES
III - MAIN PROPERTIES OF MEASURES.
04.06 m is non-decreasing : If A ⊂B, then m(A) ≤m(B).
Besides, If A ⊂B, and m(B) is ﬁnite, that m(B \ A) = m(B) −m(A∪
B).
04.07 A measure m ﬁnite if and only if only if m(Ω) is ﬁnite if and
only if m is bounded.
04.08 If a measure m is ﬁnite, then for any measurable sets A and B,
then m(B \ A) = m(B) −m(A ∩B).
04.09 A measure m is σ-sub-additive.
04.10 A measure m is continuous in the following sense :
(a) Let (An)(n≥0) be a non decreasing sequence of elements of A and
let us denote A = S
n≥0
An. Then m(An) broadly increases to m(A) as
n →+∞.
(b) Let (An)(n≥0) be a non increasing sequence of elements of A and
let us denote A = T
n≥0
An such :
∃n0 ≥0, m(An0) < +∞.
Then m(An) broadly decreases to m(A) as n →+∞.
04.11 Non-negative ﬁnite and non-negative countable linear combina-
tions of measures are measures.
04.12 A probability measure (or simply a probability) is a measure P
such that P(Ω) = 1. If P is a probability measure on A, (Ω, A, P) is
called a probability space.
===== page 225 =====
5. MEASURES
215
IV - Simple examples of measure.
(04.13) The null measure. On any measurable space(Ω, A), we have
the null measure z which assigns the null value to all measurable set :
∀A ∈A,
z(A) = 0.
(04.14a) Dirac measure.
Consider a measurable space (Ω, A), one point ω0 of Ωand a non-
negative real number α, the application δω0,α deﬁned on A by
δω0,α(A) = α1A,
A ∈A,
is a measure, called the delta-measure concentrated at ω0 with mass α.
if α = 1, we simply write δω0 and call it the delta-measure concentrated
at ω0.
(04.14b) Counting measure.
The counting measure is a very important one in this theory from
a conceptual point of view. It is usually deﬁned on a countable set
Ω= {ω1, ω2, ...} endowed with the σ-algebra P(Ω), like N the set of
nonnegative integers, Z the set of all integers, Q the set of rational
numbers, etc.
By Point 04.11, the sum of all the δωj’s
ν =
X
j≥1
δωj
is a measure such that for any A ⊂Ω,
ν(A) =
X
j≥1
δωj(A).
We may quickly see that for any A ⊂Ω,
ν(A) = Card(A).
The name of counting measure comes from the fact that ν counts the
number of elements of subsets of a countable set Ω.

NOTA-BENE. Except this important measure, other nontrivial mea-
sures are either constructed or derived from constructed measures.

V - IMAGE MEASURE AND INDUCED MEASURE.
Let (Ω, A, m) be a measure space and let X : (Ω, A, m) 7→(E, B) be a
measurable application from (Ω, A, m) to the measure space (E, B).
We may transfer the measure m deﬁned on (Ω, A) to a new one de-
noted mX deﬁned on (E, B) by means of the inverse image function
X−1 of X.
4.15 Image Measure. The application mX deﬁned on B by
mX(B) = m(X−1(B)),
B ∈B,
is a measure on (E, B) called image measure (of m by X).
Important remark.
Despite of its simplicity, image measures are
very important in probability theory.
The image of a probabibility
measure P by a measurable application X is called the probability
law, denoted PX. Finding probability laws of measurable applications
is the top objective in that theory.
4.16 Induced Measure.
(Please, revise Exercise 7, Doc 01-02 of
Chapter 1 before proceeding).
Let A be a measurable set on the measure space (Ω, A, m). The appli-
cation mA deﬁned on (A, AA) by
mA(B) = m(A ∩B),
B ⊂A,
is a measure on (A, AA) called the induced measure on the induced
measurable space.

5. MEASURES
VI - NULL SETS AND ALMOST-EVERYWHERE PROP-
ERTIES.
(A) Null sets.
04.17 A measurable set N is said to be a m-null set if and only if
m(N) = 0.
Immediate properties.
(1) A measurable set that is included in a m-null set is a m-null set.
(2) A ﬁnite or a countable union m-null sets is a m-null set.
(B) Almost everywhere true properties.
04.18 Let P(ω) a proposition depending on ω. We say that P is true
almost-everywhere (a.e.) if and only if the set
{ω, P(ω) is false}
is measurable and
m({ω, P(ω) is false}) = 0.
This is denoted by : P is true almost-everywhere, abbreviated into :
P a.e.
Measure Space Reduction Property : If each assertion of a count-
able family of assertions (Pn, n ≥0) deﬁned on the measure space
(Ω, A, m) holds almost everywhere, then all the assertions simultane-
ously hold on a measurable subspace Ω0, which is complement of a
null-set, that is m(Ω0) = 0.
In situations where we have a a countable family of a.e.
true as-
sertions (Pn, n ≥0), we may and usually assume the they are all
true everywhere, but putting ourselves on the induced measure space
(Ω0, AΩ0, mΩ0) (See Point 04.16 above). This change does not aﬀect
the measure of any measurable set A, since
===== page 229 =====
5. MEASURES
219
m(A) = mΩ0(A).

(C) Equivalence classes of measurable applications.
04.19a Denote by L0(Ω, A) the class of all real-valued and measurable
applications deﬁned on (Ω, A). We write L0 in short.
Consider two elements f and g of L0, the sets
(f = g) = {ω ∈Ω, f(ω) = g(ω)} and (f̸ = g) = {ω ∈Ω, f(ω)̸ = g(ω)}
are measurable.
04.19b We may deﬁne the binary relation R on L2
0 deﬁned by
fRg ⇔m(f̸ = g) = 0,
that is, f and g are equal outside of a m-null set, denoted also by :
f = g almost-everywhere (a.e.)
This is an equivalence relation. The equivalence classes of this relation
are :
˙f = {g ∈L0, g = f a.e.}.
04.20 The set of all equivalence classes is denoted
L0(Ω, A, m).

(D) Complete measure.
We know that a measurable subset of m-null set is a m-null set. We
cannot say anything for that a subset of a m-null if we do not know if
this subset is measurable or not. We have the following deﬁnition.
04.21 A measure space is complete if and only if any subset of a null
set is measurable and then is a null set.
04.22 Fortunately, it is possible to extend non-complete measures to
complete measures in the following way. Assume that (Ω, A, m) is not
complete. Set
N = {N ⊂Ω, ∃B m-null set, N ⊂B}).
Deﬁne
b
A = {A ∪N, A ∈A, N ∈N },
and next deﬁne the application
bm : b
A →R+
such that
bm(A ∪N) = m(A),
for A ∈A, N ∈N .
We have :
(1) b
A is a σ-algebra including A,
(2) bm is a measure on (Ω, b
A),
(3) the measure space (Ω, b
A, bm) is a complete measure space and m is
a restriction of ˜m on A.
We may and do make the following conclusion : Any measure may
be considered as a complete measure and, from now on, in
any measure space; subsets of null-sets are null sets.

VII - Outer measures
An application µ deﬁned on P(Ω) is an exterior measure if and only if
it is positive, non-decreasing, σ−sub-additive and assigns the value 0
to ∅, that is we have the following properties

The The class of µ-measurable sets
A = {A ∈P(Ω) : ∀D ⊂Ω, µ(D) = µ(AD) + µ(AcD)},

5. MEASURES
DOC 04-03 : Carath´eodory’s Theorem and appli-
cation to the Lebesgue-Stieljes Measures.
This document states main construction tool of measure : the Caratheodory’s
Theorem. Here, what is important is to understand it and to learn how
to use it, in the nontrivial case of Lebesgue measure’s construction. It
is recommended that the learners discuss it about in a session of about
thirty minutes.
Part I : Statement of Carath´eodory’s Theorem.
Carath´eodory’s Theorem allows extension of the measure on the alge-
bra to the σ-algebra it generates. It is stated as follows.
04.26 Main form of Caratheodory’s Theorem.
Let m be a proper and non-negative application deﬁned from an alge-
bra C of subsets of Ωto R+. If m is σ-additive with respect to C, then
it is extensible to a measure m0 on the σ-algebra A = σ(C) generated
by C. If m is σ-ﬁnite on C, the extension is unique and σ-ﬁnite.
Some times, it is not easy to prove that the application m is σ-additive
with respect to C. We use the following weaker form.
04.27 Weaker versions.
Using Exercise 13 in Doc 04-02 allows to replace the σ-additivity of m
by the additivity and the σ-sub-additivity to give the second version
below. As well, for ﬁnite measures, the results in Exercise 14 in Doc
04-02 lead to third version.
04.27a Version 2.
Let m be a proper and non-negative application deﬁned from an alge-
bra C of subsets of Ωto R+. If m is additive and σ-sub-additive with
respect to C, then it is extensible to a measure m0 on the σ-algebra
===== page 247 =====
5. MEASURES
237
A = σ(C) generated by C. If m is σ-ﬁnite on C, the extension is unique
and σ-ﬁnite.
When m is ﬁnite, we have to the third one.
04.27b Version 3. Let m : C →R+, be a proper non-negative and
ﬁnite application. Suppose that m is ﬁnitely-additive on C and contin-
uous at ∅, i.e., if (An)n≥1 is a sequence of subsets of C non-increasing
to ∅, then m(An) ↓0, as n ↑+∞,
Then, m extensible to a measure on σ(C).
04.28 Construction of a measure from a semi-algebra.
In some interesting cases, we may begin to construct a proper non-
negative and additive application on a semi-algebra S and to extend
it on the algebra C = a(S) generated S and further, to extend it to
σ-algebra A generated by both C and S.
The method is explained by the results in Exercise 14 in Doc 04-02. A
proper, non-negative and additive application m is deﬁned on S. It is
uniquely extended to a proper, non-negative and additive application
bm on C. If bm is σ-sub-additive, it extensible to a measure on by the
second version. But bm is σ-sub-additive if and only if m is. This gives
the advanced version of Caratheodory’s theorem.
Advanced form of Caratheodory’s Theorem.
Let S be semi-
algebra of subsets of Ω. Let m : S →R+ be a proper non-negative and
additive. It it is σ-sub-additive, then it is extensible to a measure on
A = a(S).
If m is σ-ﬁnite with respect to S, then the extension is unique and
σ-ﬁnite.
===== page 248 =====
238

Part II : Main application to the Lebesgue-Stieljes Measure
on R.
Let us begin to deﬁne distribution functions.
04.29 Deﬁnition A non-constant function F : R 7−→R is a if and
only if
(1) it assigns non-negative lengths to intervals, that is for any real
numbers a abd b such that a ≤b, we have
∆a,bF = F(b) −F(a) ≥0. (∆F)
(2) it is right-continuous at any point t ∈R.
Warning and Remark The condition (∆F) incidentally means that
F is non-decreasing on R. But, keep using the saying that assigns
non-negative lengths to intervals or intervals of R have non-negative
F-lengths. This approach will help you to move smoothly to higher
dimension.
The application of the Caratheodory theorem gives the following
Theorem-Deﬁnition.
04.30 (a) For any distribution function F, there exists a unique mea-
sure on (R, B(R)) such that such that any real numbers a and b satis-
fying a ≤b, we have
(0.1)
λF(]a, b]) = ∆F(]a, b]) = F(b) −F(a).
This measure is called the Lebesgue-Stieljes measure associated with
the distribution function F.
By applying this to the special case where F(x) = x, we get the
Lebesgue measures on (R, B(R)) as follows.
04.30 (b) The Lebesgue measure on λ, is the unique measure on
(R, B(R)) such that any real numbers a and b satisfying a ≤b, we
have
===== page 249 =====
5. MEASURES
239
(0.2)
λ(]a, b]) = b −a.
The Lebesgue measure on R is the measure that assigns to an interval
its length.
===== page 250 =====
240

The measure m deﬁned in Formula (0.3) has the following features.
(1) m is called the product measure of the measures mi, i = 1, ..., k.
(2) m is unique and σ-ﬁnite if each measure mi, i = 1, ..., k, is σ-ﬁnite.
(3) The application m deﬁned on S by Formula (0.3) is additive and
then extensible to an additive application on C = σ(S)
(4) The extension the application m, as deﬁned on S, to a measure on
A, is possible by using the Caratheodory’s Theorem when the factors
are R, or some special cases.
(5) There exists a very beautiful and general proof using integration,
even when the mi are not σ-ﬁnite. We will favor this proof that will
be given in special chapter.
(6) The product measure is very important in probability theory in
relation with the notion of independence.

Exercise 1. Let (E, B(E), m) be a ﬁnite measure ((E, B(E)) is a Borel
space. Show that any other measure that is equal to m on the class
of of open sets, or on the class of closed sets is equal to m on B(E).
Conclusion : A ﬁnite measure on a Borel space us regular, i.e, it is de-
termined by its values on the open sets, by its values on the closed sets.
Exercise 2. (Approximation of a ﬁnite measure by its values on open
and closed sets in a metric space). Let (S, d, m) be a ﬁnite measure on
the metric space (S, d) endowed with its Borel σ-algebra. Let A ∈B(S).
Show the assertion P(A) : for any ε > 0, there exist a closed set F and
an open set G such that : F ⊂A ⊂G and m(G \ F) < ε.
Hint. Proceed as follows.
Question (a) Let A be closed set. Fix δ > 0 and set the δ-dilation or
δ-enlargement of G as
Gδ = {x, d(x, A) < δ}.
(a) Show that Gδ is an open set (or you may skip this as a result of
topology).
Let (δn)n≥1 be sequence of positive number such that δn ↓+∞as
n ↑+∞.
(b) By using the continuity of m with the sequence (Gδn)n≥1, and by
noticing that A ⊂Gδn for any n, show that for any ε > 0, there exists
δ > 0 such that
m(Gδ \ A) < ε.
(c) Deﬁne by D the class of measurable sets A such that for any ε > 0,
there exist a closed set F and an open set G such that : F ⊂A ⊂G
and m(G \ F) < ε.
Show that D is a σ-algebra including the class F of closed sets. Con-
clude that the assertion P holds.

PART A : General properties on measures and additive sets

Exercise 1. Let A be σ-algebra of subsets of Ω. Let m be an applica-
tion from A to R+. Suppose that we have :
(ii) For all sequence (An)(n≥0) of pairwise disjoint elements of A, the
σ-additivity formula holds
m(
X
n≥0
An) =
X
n≥0
m(An)
holds. Show that the two assertions are equivalent :
(i1) m(∅)=0.
(i2) m is a proper application constantly equal to +∞, that is :
∃A ∈A, m(A) < +∞.
Show why the results you obtain still hold if m is only ﬁnitely additive
on an algebra.
Conclusion. The results of the exercise justify the equivalence of the
deﬁnitions of a measure in Points (04-01a) and (04-01b).
SOLUTIONS. We have to prove the equivalence between {(i1), (ii)}
and {(i2), (ii)}. But (i1) obviously implies (i2) and hence {(i1), (ii)} ⇒
{(i2), (ii)}. It remains to prove that {(i2), (ii)} ⇒{(i1), (ii)}. Now
suppose that {(i2) and (ii)}. We only need to prove that (i1) holds.
But by (ii), there exists A in A such that m(A) is ﬁnite. Since A and
∅are two elements of A and m is σ-additive and hence additive, we we
have
m(A) = m(A + ∅) = m(A) + m(∅).
Since m(A) is ﬁnite, we get
m(∅) = m(A) −m(A) = 0.
===== page 261 =====
5. MEASURES
251
So we have the equivalence between {(i1), (ii)} and {(i2), (ii)}. We
only use the ﬁnite additivity. So the result is also valid in the present
from : For an additive application on an algebra, the assertion that m
is proper is equivalent to m(∅) = 0. ■
Exercise 2. Let (Ω, A, m) be measure space. Show the following prop-
erties.
(1) m is non-decreasing : If A ⊂B, then m(A) ≤m(B). Besides,
show, if m(B) is ﬁnite, that m(B \ A) = m(B) −m(A ∩B).
Hint : Use and justify B = (A ∩B) + (B \ A) and use the additivity
and the non-negativity of a measure.

if m is bounded.

SOLUTIONS.
(1). Let (A, B) ∈A2. With the help of a simple diagram, we can see
that A = (A ∩B) + (B \ A). By additivity, we have
m(B) = m(A ∩B) + m(B \ A).
From that formula, we see that, if A ⊂B, we have m(B) = m(A) +
m(B \ A) and next, m(B) ≥m(A) since m(B \ A) ≥0.
Next, we mat derive from it, if m(B) is ﬁnite, that m(B\) is ﬁnite by
the ﬁrst conclusion and ﬁnally, we have
m(B \ A) = m(B) −m(A ∩B).
(2). Let m be ﬁnite. Then m(Ω) is ﬁnite and, by Point (1) of this
Exercise, we have for all A ∈A, m(A) ≤m(Ω). Hence, m is bounded
by m(Ω).
In the other side, if m is bounded, say by M < +∞, we get that
m(A) ≤M for all A ∈A. Hence m is ﬁnite.
Conclusion : The measure m ﬁnite if and only if it is bounded, and in
that case, m(Ω) is the maximum value of m.

Exercise 8. A measure space (Ω, A, m) is said to be complete if and
only if all subsets on m-null sets are measurable, and the then null
m-sets.
Such a notion of complete measure is very pratical. Indeed, not being
able to measure a set one knows it is part of m-null set is pathological.
This opposes to naive belief that any part of a negligeable (null set)
object should be negligeable too. The following exercise aims at ﬁxing
this problem.

Exercise 10. Let (Ω, A) a measurable space such that A is generated
by an algebra C of subsets of Ω. Show that two ﬁnite measure which

mi’s for non-increasing sequences, we do not have to worry about the

This means that m1 = m2 on A.
Exercise 11. (The λ-π Lemma) Let (Ω, A) a measurable space such
that A is generated by an π-system P containing Ω. Show that two
ﬁnite measures which equal on P, are equal (on A).
Hint : Let m1 and m2 be two ﬁnite measures on (Ω, A) equal on P.

Exercise 4 in DOC 01-04 to conclude.
===== page 283 =====
5. MEASURES

SOLUTIONS. Let us show that D is a Dynkin system.
(i) Let us show that Ω∈P. Since Ωis P and since m1 = m2 on P, we
have m1(Ω) = m2(Ω) and hence : Ω∈P.
(ii) Let us show that if A ∈D, B ∈D and A ⊂B, we have B \ A ∈D.
Indeed, suppose that A ∈D, B ∈D and A ⊂B. By Point (1) of
Exercise 2 of this series, we have for each o ∈{1, 2},
mi(B \ A) = mi(B) −mi(A)
and since A and B are in D, we have
m1(B \ A) = m1(B) −m1(A) = m2(B) −m2(A) = m2(B \ A).
Thus m1(B \ A) = m2(B \ A) and, by this, B \ A ∈D.
(iii) Let us show that D is stable under countable disjoint unions. Let
(An)(n≥n) a sequence of pairwise disjoint elements of D and let
A =
X

the Dynkin system generated by P, which, by Exercise 4 in DOC 01-
04, is equal to σ(P) = A. By construction, D is in P. As a conclusion,
we have
{A ∈A, m1(A) = m2(A)} = A.
This means that m1 = m2 on A.

Exercise 2. Let (R, B(R)) be the Borel space.
Question (a) Consider the Lebesgue measure λ on R.
(a1) Show that for any x ∈R, λ({x}) = 0.
(a2) Show that for countable A subset of R, λ({A}) = 0.
Question (b) Consider a Lebesgue-Stieljes measure λF on R where F
is a distribution function.
(b1) Show that for any x ∈R,
===== page 299 =====
5. MEASURES
289
λF({x}) = F(x) −F(x −0),
where F(x −0) is the left-hand limit of F at x.
Deduce from this that λF({x}) if and only if F is continuous at x.
(b2) Show that for any λF({x})̸ = 0 for at most a countable number

Solutions.
Question (a).
(a1). For all x ∈R, we have
{x} =
\
n≥1
]x −1/n, x].
Actually, we have ]x −1/n, x] ↓x. By the continuity of the measure,
we have λ(]x −1/n, x]) = 1/n ↓λ({x}). So, λ(x) = 0. We also may
say that
∀δ > 0, {x} ⊂]x −δ, x + δ],
which, by the non-decreasingness of the measure, implies that
∀δ > 0, 0 ≤λ({x}) ≤2δ.
By letting δ ↓0, we get that λ({x}) = 0.
(a2). If A is countable, it is a countable union of the singletons of its
elements, which are λ-null sets. So it is a λ-null set by Exercise 7 in
Doc 04-02 of this chapter.
Question (b).
(b1). For all x ∈R, from
===== page 300 =====
290
5. MEASURES
{x} =
\
n≥1
]x −1/n, x].
We get that λF(]x −1/n, x]) = F(x) −F(x −1/n) ↓λF({x}). Since F
is non-decreasing and F(x) is ﬁnite, the limit of F(x −h) as h tends
to zero by positive values exists, is bounded below by F(x) and is the
monotone limit of F(x −h) as h ↓0. By deﬁnition, this limit is the
left-limit F(x−0) at x. As a sub-limit, we have F(x−1/n) ↓F(x−0)
as n ↑+∞. We conclude that
λF({x}) = F(x) −F(x −0).
Since F is right-continuous, the fact λF({x}) = 0 implies that F(x −
0) = F(x), that is F is left-continuous and thus, continuous. Thus, F
is continuous at x if and only if λF({x}) = 0.
(b2). By Exercise 1 in Doc 03-06 in Chapter 3, the monotone func-
tion F has at most a countable number of discontinuity points. Hence
λF({x})̸ = 0 for at most a countable of real numbers x.

5. MEASURES
Vitali’s construction of a non measurable subset on R.
We are going to exhibit a non-measurable subset on R when endowed
with the usual sigma-algebra.
We will focus on ]0, 1] on which we
use the circular translation, in place of the straight translation by x :
t֒ →t + x on the whole real line.
We begin to deﬁne the circular translation and show that the Lebesgue
measure is invariant by circular translations. Next we use the translation-
invariance of the Lebesgue measure to exhibit a non-measurable subset
of R.
1 - Circular translation. Let R ∋x > 0, 0 < x < 1. Deﬁne the
circular translation on ]0, 1] by
Tx :
]0, 1]
−→
]0, 1]
y֒
→

z = y + x
if
y + x ≤1
z=x + y −1
if
y + x > 1 .
We have to understand how this circular translation is deﬁned. If y +x
is still in ]0, 1], we let is as such. But, if is exceeds 1, that is y + x > 1,
we associate y to x + y −1. That is : we want to y to y + x. When
we reach 1, we go back to 0 and continue our process and we stop at
y + x −1 : the total increase will be (1 −y) plus (x + y −1), that is
exactly x.
We may simply write
Tx(y) = (y + x)1(y≤1−x) + (y + x −1)1(y>1−x).
This function is a bijection and its inverse is T−x deﬁned by
T−x :
]0, 1]
−→
]0, 1]
z֒
→

y = z −x
if
z −x > 0
y = z −x + 1
if
z −x ≤0 ,
that is
T−x(z) = (z −x)1(z>x) + (z −x + 1)1(z≤x).
Image measure of the Lebesgue Measure by T−x.

297

(2) Cases 1 and 4 are easy to see. Let us have a close sight at Case

lation. Now, since y′ + x exceeds 1, the circular translation gives ﬁrst
the interval ]y + x, 1] and we transfer the exceedance x + y′ −1 to
form the interval ]0, y′ + x −1]. We may see that y′ + x −1 ≤y + x,
otherwise we would have (y′ +x−1 > y +x ⇐⇒y′ −y > 1) and this is
impossible. Then the intervals ]0, y′ + x −1] and ]y + x, 1] are disjoint
and its Lebesgue measure y′ −y.
We successfully checked that λx(]y, y′]) = y′−y for all intervals ]y, y′] ⊂
]0, 1], with y ≤y′. Then λx = λ for all 0 ≤x < 1.

Proofs of the three points.
Point (i) holds by the deﬁnition of circular translations.
Point (ii). let z ∈]0, 1], then its class is represented by one element its
H, say x. Then z and x are in relation, that is, z −x = r ∈] −1, 1[
and r rational. Thus z = x + r = x ⊕r ∈H ⊕r. Point (ii) is proved.
Point (iii). Suppose we can ﬁnd one element in the intersection between
H ⊕r1 and H ⊕r2 with r1̸ = r2. So z may be written as z = x1 + r1
with x ∈H and z = x2 + r2 with x2 ∈H. We have that x1 and x2
are not equal since this would lead to r1 = r2, and this is contrary to
the assumptions. Now suppose that x1 and x2 are diﬀerent. We have
z = x1 + r1 = x2 + r2 leads to x1 −x2 = r1 −r2. So x1 and x2 are
in relation. We have that x1 and x2 diﬀerent and belong to the same
equivalence class. This is not possible since we have exactly one point
of each equivalence in H. So x1 and x2 neither equal nor equal. This is
absurd. So the intersection between H ⊕r1 and H ⊕r2 is empty when

r1̸ = r2.
Put together these three points to conclude.
3 - Conclusion : H is not measurable.
Suppose H is measurable.
So the sets H ⊕r, r ∈Q∩]0, 1[, form a
countable family of measurable sets, and then
λ(]0, 1]) =
X
r∈Q∩]0,1[
λ(H ⊕r).
Since λ(H ⊕r) = λ(H) ∈[0, 1] for r ∈Q∩]0, 1[, we get
1 = Card(Q∩]0, 1[) × λ(H) = +∞× λ(H).
There exists no real number λ(H) ∈[0, 1] such that 1 = +∞× λ(H).
So the assumption that H is measurable is impossible.
Conclusion : the set H is not measurable.

Exercise 1. Let (E, B(E), m) be a ﬁnite measure ((E, B(E)) is a Borel
space. Show that any other measure that is equal to m on the class
of of open sets, or on the class of closed sets is equal to m on B(E).
Conclusion : A ﬁnite measure on a Borel space us regular, i.e, it is de-
termined by its values on the open sets, by its values on the closed sets.
Solution. The σ-algebra B(E) is generated by the class of open sets
which is a π-class containing the full space E. So, by the π −λ lemma
(See Exercise 11 in Doc 04-02 of this Chapter), two ﬁnite measure
equal on the class of open sets are equal. The reasoning is the same for
the class of closed sets, which is also a π-class containing the full space
E and which generates B(E).

Let m be a measure on the Borel sets of R such that for any any t ∈R,
(0.4)

The function Fm : R 7−→R deﬁned by
(0.5)
t 7→Fm(t)
has the following properties.
Claim 1. Fm assigns non-negative lengths to intervals, that is.,
(0.6)
t ≤s =⇒∆Fm(]t, s]) = Fm(s) −Fm(t) ≥0.
Here we have to pay attention to the terminology. We say that ∆Fm(]t, s] =
Fm(s) −Fm(t) is the length of the interval ]t, s] by Fm. It is a pure co-
incidence that 0.6 means that Fm is non-decreasing in this particular
case. In higher order spaces, this notion of increasingness will disap-
pear while the notion of positive lengths will be naturally extended to
that positive areas and generally to positive volumes.
Now the proof of 0.6 is immediately seen by remarking that
(t ≤s) ⇒(] −∞, t]) ⊆] −∞, s])
and (0.6) holds.

Fm(tn) ↓Fm(t)
as tn ↓t
⇔(∀(1 ≤i ≤k), tn

Proof.
Let tn ↓t. We get
] −∞, tn] =
↓] −∞, t].

Since m(]−∞, tn]) is ﬁnite for all n’s, we get by right-continuity of the

Fm(tn) = m(] −∞, tn]) ↓m(] −∞, t]) = Fm(t).

] −∞, t] ↓∅as t ↓−∞and m(] −∞, t]) < +∞for all t ∈R

Definition 1. A non-constant function F : R 7−→R is a distribu-

(1) it assigns non-negative lengths to intervals
and

not required that F(−∞) = 0. The second deﬁnition is more restrictive

Definition 2. A non-negative function F : R 7−→R is a prob-

(1) it assigns non-negative lengths to intervals,
(2) it is right-continuous
and

We conclude by saying that a measure m which assigns ﬁnite values
to bounded above intervals generates the distribution functions Fm.
A probability measure generates the probability distribution functions
Fm.

Doc 04-10 : Appendix : Proof the Carath´eodory’s
Theorem.
This text gives the full details of the proof of the theorem. The stu-
dents may skip this for a ﬁrst reading. It is recommended to read the
document with the help of an assistant. This document also introduces
to outer measures.
Part I : Statement of Carath´eodory’s Theorem.
Carath´eodory’s Theorem allows extension of the measure on the alge-
bra to the σ-algebra it generates. It is stated as follows.
Main form of Caratheodory’s Theorem.
Let m be a non-negative application not constantly equal to +∞on
an algebra C of subsets of Ω. If m is σ-additive, then it is extendable
to measure m0 on the σ -algebra A = σ(C) generated by C. If m is
σ-ﬁnite on C, the extension is unique.
We also have other versions.
Version 2. Let m : C →R, be a non-negative application not con-
stantly equal to +∞such that
(i) m is ﬁnitely-additive on C, i.e.
∀(A, B) ∈C2, m(A+B)=m(A)+m(B)
and
(ii) m is σ-sub-additive on C, i.e., if an element A of C is decomposed
as a sum of elements Ai of C,
A =
∞
X
i=1
Ai.
===== page 328 =====
318
5. MEASURES
Then, we have
m(A) ≤
∞
X
i=1
m(Ai).
Then, m is extendable to a measure on σ(C).
Version 3. Let m : C →R, be a non-negative and ﬁnite application
not constantly equal to +∞such that
(i) m is ﬁnitely-additive on C, i.e.
∀(A, B) ∈C2, m(A+B)=m(A)+m(B)
and
(ii) m is continuous at ∅, i.e., if (An)n≥1 is a sequence of subsets of C
non-increasing to ∅, then m(An) ↓0, as n ↑+∞.
Then, m extendable to a measure on σ(C).
The proof of the theorem will be derived from outer measures. So, we
are going to study outer measures before we come back to the proof.

II - Outer measures.
An application m0 deﬁned on P(Ω) is an exterior measure if and only

value at ∅, that is

For every A ⊂B,
