m0(∅) = 0.
Now, we are going to construct a σ-algebra A0 of subsets of (Ω, A) such
that the restriction of m0 on A is a measure. To begin with, we deﬁne
m0-measurability.
A subset A of Ωis said to be m0-measurable if and only if for every
subset D of Ω,
(0.14)
m0(D) ≥m0(AD) + m0(AcD).
This formula is enough for establishing the m0-measurability, but we
have more. Because of sub-additivity, we have
m0(D) = m0(AD + AcD) ≤m0(AD) + m0(AcD)
so that that for every subset A m0-measurable, we have
(0.15)
m0(D) = m0(AD) + m0(AcD).
Now consider the A0 the collection of all m0-measurable subsets. This
is our candidate. We are going to proof that A0 is a σ-algebra. But we
ill need this intermediate result.

(P) If A and B are two disjoints elements of A0 then A + B ∈A0 and
m0(A + B) = m0(A) + m0(B).
By using the fact that A ∈A0, we have for any subset D of Ω, we have

Doc 04-11 Application of the exterior measure to
Lusin’s theorem.
Introduction By applying the proof of the Caratheodory measure for
the Lebesgues measure on R, we get this interesting property which
approximates any Borel set with ﬁnite measure to a sum on intervals.
Lusin’s theorem.
Let be a Borel subset A of R such that its Lebesgues measure is ﬁnite,
that is λ(E) < +∞. Then, for any ε > 0, there exists a ﬁnite union K
of bounded intervals such that
λ(E∆K) < ε.
Solution. We have to go back to the proof of the Theorem of Caratheodory
which justiﬁed the existence which the Lebesgue measure.
Recall that the Lebesgue measure is uniquely deﬁned on the class of
intervals
I = {]a, b], −∞≤a ≤b < +∞}

λ(]a, b]) = b −a.

class of ﬁnite sums of disjoints intervals. It is easily proved that λ is
additive on I and is readily extensible to a an additive application on
C, that we always denote by λ. The broad extension of λ to a measure
on a σ-algebra A0 including C may be done by the method of the outer
measure, deﬁned as follows, for any subset A of R :

6. INTEGRATION
Doc 05-01 : Integration with respect to a measure
- A summary.
Summary : The integration with respect to a measure is the most
general theory of integration. It generalizes the Riemman integral on
compact intervals on R. This measure in an abstract one, more ori-
ented to theoretical purposes.
I - Construction of the integral and Main properties.
The main point is that this measure is constructed. This means the
the construction process should be mastered and never be forgotten.
The construction is achieved in three points. This leads to an easy
handling of integration.
05-01 Deﬁnition of the integral with respect to a measure.
Assume that we have a measure space (Ω, A, m).
We are going to
construct the integral of a real-value measurable function f : (Ω, A) →

by

sequence which is used in the deﬁnition.

In the general case, let us use the decomposition of f into its positive
part and its negative part :
f = f + −f −|f| = f + + f −, and f +f −= 0, (PPPN)
where f + = max(0, f) and f −= max(0, −f), which are measurable,
form the unique couple of functions such that Formulas (PPPN) holds.

respect to a measure m exists if only if : either it is of constant sign
or the integral of its positive part or its negative part is ﬁnite.

III - Spaces of integrable functions.
05-07a Space Lp(Ω, A, m), p ≥1.
Lp(Ω, A, m) is the space of all real-valued and measurable functions f
deﬁned on Ωsuch that |f|p is integrable with respect to m, that is
Lp(Ω, A, m) = {f ∈L0(Ω, A)
Z
|f|p dm < +∞}.
Recall : L0(Ω, A) is the class of all real-valued and measurable func-
tions deﬁned on Ωas in Point 4.19a in Doc 04-01 of Chapter 5, page
220.
05-07b Space of m-a.e. bounded functions L∞(Ω, A, m).
L∞(Ω, A, m) is the class of all real-valued and measurable functions
which are bounded m-a.e., i.e,
L∞(Ω, A, m) = {f ∈L0(Ω, A)0, ∃C ∈R+, |f| ≤C m −a.e}.
05-07c Equivalence classes.
When we consider only the equivalence classes pertaining to the equiv-
alence relation introduced in Point 4.19b in Doc 04-01 in Chapter 5,
page 210, we get the quotient spaces
Lp(Ω, A, m) = Lp(Ω, A, m)/R.
that is,
Lp(Ω, A, m) = { ˙f, f ∈L0(Ω, A),
Z
|f|p dm < +∞}
and
L∞(Ω, A, m) = L+∞(Ω, A, m)/R
that is
L∞(Ω, A, m) = { ˙f, fL0(Ω, A), |f| m −bounded}.

Measure Space Reduction Property .
We may complete the
ﬁrst statement this principle (See page 218 in Chapter 5) by saying
: In situations where we have a a countable family of a.e. true as-
sertions (Pn, n ≥0), we may and usually assume the they are all
true everywhere, but putting ourselves on the induced measure space
(Ω0, AΩ0, mΩ0). This change does not aﬀect neither the measure values
of measurable sets nor the values of integrals of real-valued functions
whenever they exist (See Solutions of Exercise 10 of Doc 05-02 in Doc
05-05, page 375).
05-08 Norms on Lp.
The space Lp(Ω, A, m), 1 ≤p < +∞, is equipped with the norm
||f||p = (
Z
|f|pdm)1/p.
The space L+∞(Ω, A, m) is equipped with the norm
||f||∞= inf{C ∈R+, |f| ≤C m −.a.e}.
05-9 Banach spaces.
For all p ∈[1, +∞], Lp(Ω, A, m)(+, ., ||f||p) is a Banach space.
L2(Ω, A, m)(+, ., ||f||2) is a Hilbert space.

Doc 05-04 :
Lebesgue/Riemann-Stieljes integrals
on R - Exercises.
This document is only an introduction to the comparison between the
Riemann-Stieljes Integral and the Lebesgue-Stieljes integral.
Later, we will come back to a more deep document on the same subject
after we have mastered the convergence theorems.
Reminder of the deﬁnition of the Riemann-Stieljes Integral.
The Classical Riemann-Stieljes Integral is deﬁned for bounded func-
tions on compacts intervals.
In all this text, a and b are two real
numbers such that a < b and F : [a, b] −→R is non-constant non-
decreasing function.
Deﬁnition of the Riemann-Stieljes Integral on a compact set.
Let f : ]a, b] −→R be an arbitrary function. We are going to deﬁne the
Riemann-Stieljes integral of f on ]a, b] associated with F and denoted
I =
Z b
a
f(x) dF(x).
We begin to deﬁne the Riemann-Stieljes sums. For each n ≥1, consider
a subdivision πn of ]a, b], that divides into ]a, b] into the ℓ(n) sub-
intervals
]a, b] =
ℓ(n)−1
X
i=0
]xi,n, xi+1,n],
with a = x0,n < x1,n < · · · < xℓ(n),n = b. The modulus of the subdivi-
sion πn is deﬁned by
m(πn) =
max
0≤i≤m(n)(xi+1,n −xi+1,n).
Associate to this subdivision πn an arbitrary sequence cn = (ci,n)1≤i≤ℓ(n)
such that c0,n ∈]a, x1,n] and ci,n ∈[xi,n, xi+1,n], 1 ≤i ≤ℓ(n) −1.
===== page 363 =====
6. INTEGRATION
353
We may deﬁne a sequence of Riemann-Stieljes sum associated to the
subdivision πn and the vector cn in the form
Sn(f, F, a, b, πn, cn) =
ℓ(n)−1
X
i=0
f(ci,n)(F(xi+1,n) −F(xi,n)).
Since f, F and ]a, b] are ﬁxed here, we may and do drop them in the
expression of Sn.
Deﬁnition. A bounded function f is Riemann-Stieljes integrable with
respect to F if there exists a real number I such that any sequence of
Riemann-Stieljes sums Sn(πn, cn) converges to I as n →0 whenever
m(πn) →0 as n →∞, and the number I is called the Riemann-Stieljes
of f on [a, b].
If F is the identity function, that is F(x) = x, x ∈R, I is simply called
the Riemann integral of f over [a, b] and the sums deﬁned in Formula
(RSS) are called Riemann sums.

integral.

establishing the relations for all ω ∈Ω. It is enough to work with three
cases.
If f(ω) = 0, we have f +(ω) = f −(ω) = 0 and (SF) holds for h = f +
and g = f −and (AV) also holds.
If f(ω) > 0, we have f +(ω) = f(ω) and f −(ω) = 0. You may easily
check that (SF) holds for h = f + and g = f −and (AV) also holds.
If f(ω) < 0, we have f +(ω) = 0 and f −(ω) = −f(Ω). You may easily
check that (SF) holds for h = f + and g = f −and (AV) also holds.
To prove Formulas (BO), consider the four cases :
Case 1 : f(ω) ≥0 and g(ω) ≥0 : We have

(f(ω) + g(ω))+ = f(ω) + g(ω), f +(ω) = f(ω), g+(ω) = g(ω)

S and

(f(ω) + g(ω))−= 0, f −(ω) = 0, g−(ω) = 0

.
S By looking at the details in the each of the couples of big parentheses,
we easily see that the relations in Formula (BO) hold for these ω’s.
Case 2 : f(ω) ≤0 and g(ω) ≤0. We have

(f(ω) + g(ω))+ = 0, f +(ω) = 0, g+(ω) = 0

S and

(f(ω) + g(ω))−= −f(ω) −g(ω), f −(ω) = −f(ω), g−(ω) = −g(ω)

.
S We conclude similarly.

The prove of Formula (COM) is on the fact that the function max(., .) is
coordinate-wise non-decreasing. Thus f ≤g implies that max(f, 0) ≤
max(g, 0), that is f + ≤g+. we also have f ≤g implies −f ≥−g
which leads tomax(f, 0) ≥max(g, 0), that is f −≥g−. The reverse
implication is straightforward.
(a2) Next suppose that h and g satisfy (SF).
Suppose that f(ω) = 0. We have h(ω) −g(ω) = 0 and h(ω)g(ω) = 0.
Hence, if h(ω) is not zero, so is g(ω) and f(ω) = h(ω) would not be
not null. Similarly, if g(ω) is not zero, so is h(ω) and we would have
f(ω) = −g(ω) not null. Thus, we necessarily have
h(ω) = 0 and g(ω) = 0.
S Suppose that f(ω) > 0. Since we have h(ω)g(ω) = 0, one of h(ω) and
g(ω) is zero. And in cannot be h(ω), since we would have f(ω) < 0.
Thus we have
h(ω) = f(ω) and g(ω) = 0.
S Suppose that f(ω) < 0. Since we have h(ω)g(ω) = 0, one of h(ω) and
g(ω) is zero. And in cannot be h(ω), since we would have f(ω) > 0.
Thus we have
h(ω) = 0 and g(ω) = −f(ω).
S By comparing with the results of part (a1) of this question, we may
see that h and f + on one side and g and f−have the same values in
the three possible regions f(ω) = 0, f(ω) < 0 and f(ω) > 0. S we have
the equality of theirs graphs, that is
h = f + and g = f −.

k≥1

Doc 05-07 :
Lebesgue/Riemann-Stieljes integrals
on R - Exercises with solutions.
This document is only an introduction to the comparison between the
Riemann-Stieljes Integral and the Lebesgue-Stieljes integral.
Later, we will come back to a more deep document on the same subject
after we have mastered the convergence theorems.
Reminder of the deﬁnition of the Riemann-Stieljes Integral.
The Classical Riemann-Stieljes Integral is deﬁned for bounded func-
tions on compacts intervals.
In all this text, a and b are two real
numbers such that a < b and F : [a, b] −→R is non-constant non-
decreasing function.
Deﬁnition of the Riemann-Stieljes Integral on a compact set.
Let f : ]a, b] −→R be an arbitrary function. We are going to deﬁne the
Riemann-Stieljes integral of f on ]a, b] associated with F and denoted
I =
Z b
a
f(x) dF(x).
We begin to deﬁne the Riemann-Stieljes sums. For each n ≥1, consider
a subdivision πn of ]a, b], that divides into ]a, b] into the ℓ(n) sub-
intervals
]a, b] =
ℓ(n)−1
X
i=0
]xi,n, xi+1,n],
with a = x0,n < x1,n < · · · < xℓ(n),n = b. The modulus of the subdivi-
sion πn is deﬁned by
m(πn) =
max
0≤i≤m(n)(xi+1,n −xi+1,n).
Associate to this subdivision πn an arbitrary sequence cn = (ci,n)1≤i≤ℓ(n)
such that c0,n ∈]a, x1,n] and ci,n ∈[xi,n, xi+1,n], 1 ≤i ≤ℓ(n) −1.
===== page 407 =====
6. INTEGRATION
397
We may deﬁne a sequence of Riemann-Stieljes sum associated to the
subdivision πn and the vector cn in the form
Sn(f, F, a, b, πn, cn) =
ℓ(n)−1
X
i=0
f(ci,n)(F(xi+1,n) −F(xi,n)).
Since f, F and ]a, b] are ﬁxed here, we may and do drop them in the
expression of Sn.
Deﬁnition. A bounded function f is Riemann-Stieljes integrable with
respect to F if there exists a real number I such that any sequence of
Riemann-Stieljes sums Sn(πn, cn) converges to I as n →0 whenever
m(πn) →0 as n →∞, and the number I is called the Riemann-Stieljes
of f on [a, b].
If F is the identity function, that is F(x) = x, x ∈R, I is simply called
the Riemann integral of f over [a, b] and the sums deﬁned in Formula
(RSS) are called Riemann sums.
Exercise 1.
Question (a) Let f :]a, b] −→R be a bounded and measurable function
on [a, b]. Is-it Lebesque-Stieljes integrable? If yes, give a bound of its
integral.
Question (b) Consider the function
g = 1]a,b]∩Q,
where Q is the set of rational numbers.
Consider Riemann-Stieljes sums associated to g in which the points ci,n
are chosen in Q. Justify why you can make this choice. What are the
values of these Riemann-Stieljes sums.

Riemann-Stieljes sums.
===== page 408 =====
398
6. INTEGRATION
Conclude that g is not Riemann-Stieljes integrable.
Question (c). Make a ﬁrst comparison between the two integrals.
Solution.
Question (a). Here we consider the induced Lebesgue measure λ[a,b] of
the Lebesgue measure on [a, b] which endowed with the induced with
the induced Borel σ-algebra
B([a, b]) = {B ⊂[a, b], B ∈B(R)}.
One easily shows that B([a, b]) is still generated by the semi-algebra
S([a, b]) = {a, [a, b], ]x, y], a ≤x ≤y ≤b}. The measure λ[a,b] is ﬁnite.
Thus, any constant function M = M1[a,b] is integrable, since
Z
M dλ[a,b] = Mλ[a,b]([a, b]) = M(b −a).
Next, by Formula (I2) of Point 05.06 of Doc 05-01 of this chapter, any
measurable and bounded function on [a, b] is integrable and its integral
is bounded by (b −a) times its bound.
Question (b). Consider an arbitrary subdivision of πn = (a = x0,n <
x1,n < · · · xℓ(n),n = b). Since the set of rational numbers Q is dense in R,
we may choose in any interval ]xi,n, xi+1,n[ a rational number ei,n, 0 ≤
i ≤ℓ(n)−1. With such a choice for the vector (en) = (e0,n, ..., eℓ(n)−1,n),
we have
∀n ≥1, Sn(g, F, a, b, πn, en) = 0.
Since the set of irrational numbers J is dense in R, we may choose in
any interval ]xi,n, xi+1,n[ an irrational number di,n, 0 ≤i ≤ℓ(n) −1.
With such a choice for the vector (dn) = (d0,n, ..., dℓ(n)−1,n), we have
∀n ≥1, Sn(g, F, a, b, πn, dn) = b −a.
Hence, the sequence of Riemann sums Sn(f, F, a, b, πn, cn) does not
converges as m(πn) converges to as as n →+∞for all choices of the
sequence (cn)n≥1. Thus, there is no Riemann-integral for g.

7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
Doc 06-01 : The two Main Convergence Types - A
summary.
In this tutorial, we provide the basic types of convergence and next the
fundamental convergence theorems and some of their applications.
Before you begin to read this note, we remind you to revise the appen-
dix on limits in R, called : what should not be ignored on limits in R,
in Doc 11-01 in Chapter 12. You may ﬁnd it in page 699. A perfect
mastering of inferior limits and superior limits is needed to be in ease
when working on Convergence questions of any type.
This chapter is devoted to convergence of sequences of real-valued
measurable functions. A measure space (Ω, A, m) is given for once in all
the sequel. Without any other explicit assumptions, (fn)n≥1, (gn)n≥1,
(hn)n≥1 stand for sequences of real-valued and measurable functions
deﬁned from (Ω, A, m) to R and f, g, h, etc.
are real-valued and
measurable functions deﬁned on (Ω, A, m).
The following notation is adopted :
The following fact is important before the deﬁnition of the convergence
that will comes ﬁrst.
(fn →f) = {ω ∈Ω,
fn(ω) converges to f(ω)}
and
(fn ↛f) = {ω ∈Ω,
fn(ω) does not converge to f(ω)}
with
(fn →f)c = (fn ↛f).
(06.00). If (fn)n≥1 is a sequence real-valued and measurable functions
deﬁned from (Ω, A, m) to R and if f is also a real-valued and measur-
able function, then the set (fn ↛f) us measurable.
===== page 423 =====
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
413
(06.01) - Deﬁnitions of the two main types of convergence.
(06.01a) Almost-everywhere convergence.
A sequence of measurable applications fn)n≥1 deﬁned from (Ω, A, m) to
R converges almost-everywhere to a measurable function f : (Ω, A, m)
7−→R and we denote
fn −→f, a.e.,
if and only if the elements ω ∈Ωfor which fn(ω) does not converge to
f(ω) form a null set with respect to m that is
m(fn ↛f) = 0.
remark. The a.e. convergence may may be deﬁned even if the func-
tions fn’s and f take inﬁnite values on non-null sets (with respect to
m). But if these functions are a.e. ﬁnite, more precise formulas are
given.
(06.01b) Convergence in measure.
A sequence of measurable and a.e. ﬁnite applications (fn)n≥1 deﬁned
from (Ω, A, m) to R converges in measure with respect to m to an a.e
ﬁnite measurable function f : (Ω, A, m) 7−→R, denoted
fn →m f
if and only for any ε > 0,
m(|fn −f| > ε) −→0 as n −→+∞.
Remark. The convergence in measure is only possible if the limit f
and the fn’s are a.e. since we need to make the diﬀerences fn −f. The
a.e. ﬁniteness justiﬁes this.
As remarked in the Measure Space Reduction Property seen in Doc 05-
01 in Chapter 6 (page 337), we may and do suppose that f and the
fn’s are ﬁnite on the whole space.
===== page 424 =====
414
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
NB. It is important to notice the inequality in (|fn −f| > ε) may be
strict or not.
(06.02) - Properties of the a.e. convergence.
(06.02a) - The a.e. convergence is well-deﬁned.
The deﬁnition of the a.e. limit is possible if the set (fn →f) is mea-
surable, as pointed out earlier.

(06.02b) - a.e. uniqueness of a.e. limits.
The a.e. limit is a.e. unique, meaning that :
(fn →f a.e. and fn →g a.e.)
⇒f = g a.e.

Property. Let (fn)n≥1 be a sequence of a.e. ﬁnite functions.
(fn)n≥1 is an m-a.e. Cauchy sequence if and only if (fn)n≥1 converges

(06.03) - Properties of the convergence in measure.
(06.03a) - a.e. uniqueness of limits in measure.
The limit in measure is a.e. unique, meaning that :
(fn →m f and fn →m g)
⇒f = g a.e.
(06.03b) - First comparison between the two types of limits.
Let m be a ﬁnite measure and f be a.e. ﬁnite. Let (fn)n≥1 be a se-
quence of m-a.e. functions. If fn converges to f a.e., then fn converges
to f in measure.
(06.03c) - Operations of limits in measure. We still deal with
a.e. ﬁnite functions.
The operations of limits in measure are linked with advanced notions
like boundedness in measure and other advanced types of convergence.
This explains why we cannot have the same properties as the a.e. con-
vergence. But if the limits in measure are ﬁnite real numbers, we have
equivalent results. Otherwise, you have to treat each as a a special case.
(06.03c-A) - General case.
Let fn →m f and gn →m g, a ∈R. We have :
(1) fn + gn →m f + g.
(2) afn →af
===== page 428 =====
418
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
(06.03c-B) - Finite and constant limits in measure.
Let (fn)n≥1 and (gn)n≥1 be sequences of a.e. ﬁnite functions. Let a and
b be ﬁnite real numbers. Suppose that fn →m A and gn →m B, where
A and B are ﬁnite real numbers. Let H(x, y) a continuous function of
(x, y) ∈D, where D is an open set of R2. We have :
(1) afn + bgn →m aA + bB.
(2) fngn →m AB.
(3) If B̸ = 0, then
fn/gn →m A/B.
(4) If (fn, gn)n≥1 ⊂D a.e. and (A, B) ∈D, then
H(fn, gn) →m H(A, B).
(06.03d) - Cauchy sequences in measure. Here again, we deal
with a.e. ﬁnite functions and consider a Cauchy Theory on sequences
of them. And we have the following deﬁnition and characterizations.
Deﬁnition.
A sequence (fn)n≥1 of a.e.
ﬁnite functions is Cauchy
sequence in measure if and only if, for any ε,
m(|fp −fq| > ε) →0 as (p, q) →(+∞, +∞).
Properties. Let (fn)n≥1 be a sequence of a.e. ﬁnite functions.
P1 (fn)n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence in measure if and only if (fn)n≥1 con-
verges in measure to an a.e. ﬁnite function.
P2 If (fn)n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence in measure, then (fn)n≥1 possesses
a subsequence (fnk)k≥1 and an a.e. measurable function f such that
fnk →f a.e. as k →+∞,
and
fn →m f as n →+∞.
===== page 429 =====
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
419
(06.04) - Comparison between a.e convergence and conver-
gence in measure.
(06.04b). If fn →f a.e. and m is ﬁnite, then fn →m f.
Terminology. From this, a.e. convergences are called strong in oppo-
site of convergence in measure that are called weak.
The reverse implication is not true. It is only true for a sub-sequence
as follows.
(06.04b). Let fn →m f. Then, there exists a sub-sequence (fnk)k≥1 of
(fn)n≥1 converging to f.
(06.05) - Terminology in the Frame of Probability Theory.
If the measure space is a probability space, that is m = P a probability
measure, an almost-everywhere a.e. property P becomes an almost-
sure a.s. property, that is
P(P true) = 1.
In that case :
(a) The a.e. convergence becomes the a.s. convergence.
(b) The convergence in measure becomes convergence in probability,
denoted as →P.
Since a probability measure is ﬁnite, the a.s. convergence implies the
convergence in probability and the convergence in probability implies
the existence of a subsequence converging a.s. to the limit in probabil-
ity.

(06.09c). Let f(t, ω), t ∈T a family of measurable functions indexed

(a) (Local continuity) Suppose that for some ε > 0, for ε small enough

Exercise 01. (Useful Formulas in R).
(a) Quickly check the following equivalences for an real number x or a
sequence or elements of R.
(∀ε > 0, |x| ≤ε) ⇔(∀r ∈Q and r > 0, |x| ≤r). (EQUIV 01)
(∀ε > 0, |x| ≤ε) ⇔(∀k ∈N and k ≥1, |x| ≤1/k). (EQUIV 02)
(∀A > 0, |x| ≥A) ⇔(∀k ∈N and k ≥1, |x| ≥k). (EQUIV 03)
Formula (EQUIV04a) :
(∀A > 0, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≥A)
⇔
(∀k ∈mathbbN and k ≥1, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≥k).
Formula (EQUIV04b) :
(∀A > 0, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≤−A)
⇔
(∀k ∈N and k ≥1, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≥−k).
Formula (EQUIV05) :
(∀ε > 0, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≤ε)
⇔
(∀k ∈N and k ≥1, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≤1/k).
etc.

Exercise 3.
General Hint. In each question, use the Measure Space Reduction
property (see pages 218 and 337) and combine to others assumptions
to place yourself in a space Ω0, whose complement is a null-set, and on
===== page 442 =====
432
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
which all the a.e. properties hold for each ω ∈Ω0.
Let (fn)n≥1 and (gn)n≥1 be sequences of real-valued measurable func-
tions such that fn →f a.e. and gn →g a.e.. Let a and b be ﬁnite real
numbers. Let H(x, y) a continuous function of (x, y) ∈D, where D is
an open set of R2. Show that the following assertion hold true :
(1) The a.e. limit of (fn)n≥1, if it exists, is unique a.e..
(2) If af + bg is a.e. deﬁned, then afn + bgn →af + bg a.e.
(3) If fg is a.e. deﬁned, then fngn →fg a.e
(4) If f/g is a.e. deﬁned, (that is g is a.e nonzero and f/g̸ = ±∞
±∞a.e.),
then
fn/gn →f/g, a.e.
(5) If (fn, gn)n≥1 ⊂D a.e. and (f, g) ∈D a.e., then
H(fn, gn) →H(f, g), a.e.
SOLUTIONS.
(1) Suppose that fn converges a.e. to both f1 and f2. Denote
Ωc
0 = (fn ↛f(1)) ∩(fn ↛f(2)).
It is clear that Ωc
0 is a null-set. Besides, for all ω ∈Ω0,
fn(ω) →f(1)(ω) and fn(ω) →f(2)(ω).
We get, by the uniqueness of the limit in R, we get that f(1) = f(2) on
Ω0, that is outside a null-set. Hence f(1) = f(2) a.e.. □
(2) By combining the hypotheses, we can ﬁnd a measurable space sub-
space Ω0 of Ωwhose complement is a m-null and, on which, we have
fn →f a.e. and gn →g and af + bg is well deﬁned. Then for any
ω ∈Ω0, afn(ω) + bgn(ω) is well deﬁned for large values of n and hence
(afn + bgn)(ω) →(af + bg)(ω).
===== page 443 =====
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
433
Hence afn + bgn converges to af + bg outside the null-set Ωc
0. □
(3) By combining the hypotheses, we can ﬁnd a measurable space sub-
space Ω0 of Ωwhose complement is a m-null and, on which, we have
fn →f a.e. and gn →g and fg is well deﬁned. Then for any ω ∈Ω0,
fn(ω)gn(ω) is well deﬁned for large values of n and hence
(fngn)(ω) →(fg)(ω).
Hence fngn converges to fg outside the null-set Ωc
0. □
(4) Repeat the same argument.
(5) By combining the hypotheses, we can ﬁnd a measurable space sub-
space Ω0 of Ωwhose complement is a m-null and, on which, we have
fn →f a.e. and gn →g and all the couples (fn, gn)’s are in D and (f, g)
is in D. Then for any ω ∈Ω0, (fn(ω), gn(ω)) converges (f(ω), g(ω)) in
D By continuity of H, we get
(∀ω ∈Ω), H(fn(ω), gn(ω)) →H(f(ω), g(ω)).
Hence, H(fn, gn) converges to H(f, g) outside the null-set Ω0. ■

SOLUTIONS.
Question (a).
EQUIV01. The direct implication →is obvious. To prove the indirect
implication ←, suppose that we have
(∀r ∈Q and r > 0, |x| ≤r).
Now, let ε > 0. Since Q is dense in R, the interval ]0, ε[ intersects with
Q, that is there exists r ∈Q∩]0, ε[. Then by assumption,
|x| ≤r < ε.
Hence, we have
(∀ε > 0, |x| ≤ε). □
EQUIV02. The direct implication →is obvious. To prove the indirect
implication ←, suppose that we have
(∀k ∈N and k > 1, |x| ≤1/k).
Now, let ε > 0. Since R is Archimedes, by comparing ε > 0 and
1, there exists an integer kε > 1, that is 1/k < ε > 0.
Then by
assumption,
|x| ≤1/k < ε.
Hence, we have
(∀ε > 0, |x| ≤ε). □
EQUIV03. The direct implication →is obvious. To prove the indirect
implication ←, suppose that we have
(∀k ∈N and k > 1, |x| ≥k).
===== page 468 =====
458
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
Now, let A > 0. Since R is Archimedes, by comparing a = 1 and
A > 0, there exists an integer k > 0 such that k×a > A, that is k > A.
Then by assumption,
|x| ≥k > A.
Hence, we have
(∀A > 0, |x| ≤A). □
EQUIV04. The direct implication →is obvious. To prove the indirect
implication ←, suppose that we have
(∀k ∈N and k ≥1, ∃n0 ≥0, ∀p ≥n0 : |xp| ≥k).
(∀A > 0, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≥A)
⇔
(∀k ∈N and k ≥1, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≥k). (EQUIV 04)
Now, let ε > 0. By the argument used the solution of (EQUIV03),
consider an integer k such that k > A. Then by assumption, we have
∀p ≥n0 : |xp| ≥k > A.
Hence, we have
(∀A > 0, ∃n0 ≥0, ∀p ≥n0 : |xp| ≥A). □
EQUIV05. The direct implication →is obvious. To prove the indirect
implication ←, suppose that we have
∀k ∈N and k ≥1, ∃n0 ≥0, ∀p ≥n0 : |xp| ≤1/k.
Now, let ε > 0. By the argument used the solution of (EQUIV02),
consider an integer k such that 1/kε. Then by assumption, we have
∀p ≥n0 : |xp| ≤1/k ≤ε.
We get
(∀ε > 0, ∃n ≥0, ∀p ≥n : |xp| ≤ε). □

Question (b).
Formulas (ROS01) and (RS02). By the triangle inequality, we have
(|a| ≤ε/2) and (|b| ≤ε/2) ⇒(|a + b| ≤|a| + |b| ≤ε/2 + ε/2 = ε)
which yields
(|a| ≤ε/2) and (|b| ≤ε/2) ⇒(|a + b| ≤ε. (CROS01)
The repetition of the triangle inequality k times also gives
(∀j ∈{1, ..., k}, |aj| ≤εj)
⇒

Question (c). The formulas (OS01) and (SO02) to be proved are the
sets versions (ROS1) and (RSO2). For example, we say :
ω ∈(|f + g| > ε)
⇒
|f + g|(ω) > ε (By definition)
⇔
|f(ω) + g(ω)| > ε
⇒
|f(ω)| > ε/2 or |g(ω)| > ε/2 (By (ROS1))
⇔
ω ∈(|f| > ε/2) or ω ∈(|g| > ε/2)
⇔
ω ∈(|f| > ε/2) ∩(|g| > ε/2),

Question (5). Let the fn and f be a.e. ﬁnite. Let ε > 0. Suppose that
fn →f. By Question (4), we have m(Dε) = 0. Next, the sequence
(Dn,ε)n≥0 is non-decreasing since as n grows, the unions looses members

Dn,ε’s, that is Dε.

m(Dn,ε) ↓m(Dε) = 0. (AP1)

+∞in Formula (AP2), we get

and this is valid for any ε > 0. Hence fn →m f. □
Exercise 3.
General Hint. In each question, use the Measure Space Reduction
property (see pages 218 and 337) and combine to others assumptions
to place yourself in a space Ω0, whose complement is a null-set, and on
which all the a.e. properties hold for each ω ∈Ω0.
Let (fn)n≥1 and (gn)n≥1 be sequences of real-valued measurable func-
tions such that fn →f a.e. and gn →g a.e.. Let a and b be ﬁnite real
numbers. Let H(x, y) a continuous function of (x, y) ∈D, where D is
an open set of R2. Show that the following assertion hold true :

(3) If fg is a.e. deﬁned, then fngn →fg a.e
(4) If f/g is a.e. deﬁned, (that is g is a.e nonzero and f/g̸ = ±∞
±∞a.e.),
then
fn/gn →f/g, a.e.
(5) If (fn, gn)n≥1 ⊂D a.e. and (f, g) ∈D a.e., then
H(fn, gn) →H(f, g), a.e.
SOLUTIONS.
(1) Suppose that fn converges a.e. to both f1 and f2. Denote
Ωc
0 = (fn ↛f(1)) ∩(fn ↛f(2)).
It is clear that Ωc
0 is a null-set. Besides, for all ω ∈Ω0,
fn(ω) →f(1)(ω) and fn(ω) →f(2)(ω).
We get, by the uniqueness of the limit in R, we get that f(1) = f(2) on
Ω0, that is outside a null-set. Hence f(1) = f(2) a.e.. □
(2) By combining the hypotheses, we can ﬁnd a measurable space sub-
space Ω0 of Ωwhose complement is a m-null and, on which, we have
fn →f a.e. and gn →g and af + bg is well deﬁned. Then for any
ω ∈Ω0, afn(ω) + bgn(ω) is well deﬁned for large values of n and hence
(afn + bgn)(ω) →(af + bg)(ω).
Hence afn + bgn converges to af + bg outside the null-set Ωc
0. □
(3) By combining the hypotheses, we can ﬁnd a measurable space sub-
space Ω0 of Ωwhose complement is a m-null and, on which, we have
fn →f a.e. and gn →g and fg is well deﬁned. Then for any ω ∈Ω0,
fn(ω)gn(ω) is well deﬁned for large values of n and hence

(fngn)(ω) →(fg)(ω).

(4) Repeat the same argument.

Question (2). Let c be ﬁnite real number. Show that the sequence of
a.e. constant functions, fn = c a.e., converges in measure to f = c.
Question (3). Let fn →m f and gn →m g, a ∈R. Show that
(a) fn + gn →m f + g.
(b) afn →af.
Question (4). Suppose that fn →m A and gn →m B, where A and B
are ﬁnite real numbers. Let H(x, y) a continuous function of (x, y) ∈D,
where D is an open set of R2. Let a and b be ﬁnite real numbers. Show
the following properties.
(a) afn + bgn →m aA + bB.
(b) fngn →m AB.
(c) If B̸ = 0, then
fn/gn →m A/B.
(d) If (fn, gn)n≥1 ⊂D a.e. and (A, B) ∈D, then
H(fn, gn) →m H(f, g).
SOLUTIONS.
Question (1). Suppose that fn →m f(1) and fn →m f(2). By Formula
(OS1) in Exercise 1, we have for all k ≥1
(|f1 −f2| > 1/k) ⊂(|fn −f(1)| > 1/(2k)) ∪(|fn −f(2)| > 1/(2k)),
and hence by the sub-additivity of the measure m, we have
0 ≤m(|f1−f2| > 1/k) ≤m(|fn−f(1)| > 1/(2k))+m(|fn−f(2)| > 1/(2k)).
The right-hand member of the inequality tends to zero as n →+∞by
assumptions. By the sandwich theorem, we have for all k ≥1,
===== page 483 =====

473
m(|f(1) −f(2)| > 1/k) = 0.
Since we have (See Formula (C) in Exercise 7 in DOC 04-06, page 262,
Chapter 5),
(f(1)̸ = f(2)) =
[
k≥1
(|f(1) −f(2)| > 1/k),
we get m(f(1)̸ = f(2)) = 0, since (f(1)̸ = f(2)) is a countable union of
null-sets. This means that f(1) = f(2) a.e. □
Question (2). Let fn = c for all n ≥0, where c is a ﬁnite real numbers.
Set f = c a.e. We have for all n ≥0, for all ε > 0 :
m(|fn −f| > ε) = m(0 > ε) = m(∅) = 0.
Hence fn →m f. □
Question (3).
(a) By Formula (OS1) applied to |(fn + gn) −(f + g)| = |(fn −f) −
(gn −g)|, we have for any ε > 0 :
(|(fn + gn) −(f + g)| > ε) ⊂(|fn −f| > ε/2) ∪(|gn −g| > ε/2)
which leads to
0 ≤m(|(fn+gn)−(f +g)| > ε) ≤m(|fn−f| > ε/2)+m(|gn−g| > ε/2),
and we may conclude by applying the assumption and by using the
sandwich rule.
(b) if a = 0, afn = 0 and af = 0 both a.e. (remind that the fn’s and f
are a.e. ﬁnite) and Question (2) ensures the convergence in measure.
next, suppose that a̸ = 0, we have for any ε > 0,
0 ≤m(|afn −af| > ε) = m(|a||fn −f| > ε) = m(|fn −f| > ε/|a|).

Exercise 6.
Let (fn)n≥1 be a sequence of a.e. ﬁnite and measurable real-valued
functions.
Question (a) Suppose that the (fn)n≥1 converges in measure to an a.e.
ﬁnite, measurable and real-valued function f. Show that the sequence
is a Cauchy sequence in measure.
Question (b) Suppose that (fn)n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence in measure
and that there exists a sub-sequence (fnk)k≥1 of (fn)n≥1 converging in
measure to an a.e. function f. Show that (fn)n≥1 converges in measure
to f.
Question (c) Suppose that (fn)n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence in measure and
that there exists a sub-sequence (fnk)k≥1 of (fn)n≥1 converging a.e. to
an a.e. function f. Suppose in addition that the measure is ﬁnite.
Show that (fn)n≥1 converges in measure to f.
Hint. Combine Question (b) of the current exercise and Question (4)
if Exercise 2.
SOLUTIONS.
Question (a). By deﬁnition of the convergence in measure of (fn)n≥1
to f, we have for all ε > 0,
m(|fn −f| > ε/2) →0.
By applying Formula (OS1) of Exercise 1, we have
===== page 488 =====
478
7. INTRODUCTION TO CONVERGENCE THEOREMS AND APPLICATIONS
(|fp −fq| > ε) ⊂(|fp −f| > ε/2) ∪(|fq −f| > ε/2),
which, by the sub-additivity of the measure m, leads to
0 ≤m(|fp −fq| > ε) ≤m(|fp −f| > ε/2) + m(|fq −f| > ε/2).
By using the Sandwich rule to the formula above, we get for any ε > 0,
≤m(|fp −fq| > ε) →0, as (p, q) →(+∞, +∞),
which means that (fn)n≥1 us Cauchy sequence in measure. □
Question (b). By exploiting Formulation (OS1) in Exercise 1, we get
for any p ≥0, for any k ≥0,
0 ≤m(|fp −f| > ε) ≤m(|fp −fnk| > ε/2) + m(|fnk −f| > ε/2).
Now let n →+∞and k →+∞, m(|fp −fnk| > ε/2) goes to zero
since (fn)n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence in measure and m(|fnk −f| > ε/2)
tends to zero since the sub-sequence (fnk)k≥1 converges to f . By the
Sandwich rule, the solution is over.
Question (c).Since m is supposed ﬁnite in this question, Question (4)
of Exercise 2 implies that (fnk)k≥1 converges to f in measure. Hence,
we ﬁnd ourselves in the situation of Question (b) and the conclusion is
immediate. □

NB. In this document and in the sequel of the textbook, the Measure
Space Reduction Principle (see pages 218 and 337) which consists in
placing ourselves in a sub-measurable space Ω0 whose complement is
a null-set with respect to the working measure, and on which all the
countable a.e. properties simultaneously hold for ω ∈Ω0. By working
on the induced measure on Ω0, the values of the measures of sets and
the values of the integrals of real-valued and measurable function re-
main unchanged.

ci,n’s and F(b) −F(a) for rational values. So the Riemann-Stieljes in-
tegral of f does not exists.
(c) This concludes the Exercise.

8. FINITE PRODUCT MEASURE SPACE AND FUBINI’S THEOREM
Doc 07-01 : Finite Product Measures and Fubini’s
Theorem - A summary.
Important Advice. The learner is strongly advised to go back Doc
00-01, Doc 00-02 (Exercise 7), in Chapter 1, pages 27 and 44, for a
reminder on Cartesian products on sets, and to Point (01.08) in Doc
01-01 in Chapter 2, page 48 for revisiting the product sigma-algebra.
As well, the properties of sections of subsets in a product space and
their properties are important to know before you proceed (See Exer-
cise 3, Doc 01-06 in in Chapter 2, page 87).
Remark 1 : Here, the important product measure will come out here
as an application of the Monotone Convergence Theorem (MCT).
Remark 2 : This document will focus on the simplest product k = 2.
The extension to the general case is a matter of induction.
Remark 3 : He we focus on the ﬁnite product measure of σ-ﬁnite
measures. But after we treated all fundamental properties in details,

An interesting question is : does Equality (FU3) with ﬁnite members
implies that f is integrable. We have the following facts. One of the
member of Equality (FU3) may exists and not the other. Both may
exist, be ﬁnite and equal and at the same time, f is not integrable.
Fortunately we have the Tonelli Theorem concerning the non-negative
functions. We have to remind that the (FU) holds for non-negative
functions.
If f is non-negative and one of the members of (FU3) is ﬁnite, then f
is integrable.

Integration with respect to this measure use the Fubini’s

If f is non-negative or f is integrable, then we may proceed progres-

Z

How to read Fubini’s Theorem. First, ﬁx all the variables (ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1)
and consider the function
f(ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1, ωk)
as depending only ωk and integrate it with respect to dmk(ωk). And
the function
Z
Ωk
f(ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1, ωk) dm(ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1, ωk)dmk(ωk),
is a function of (ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1). We repeat the same procedure, by
ﬁxing (ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−2) and by integrating with respect to dmk−1(ωk−1)
so that the result is a function of (ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−3). Finally, after k −1
steps downward, we arrive at a function of ω1 and we integrate it with
respect to dm1(ω1).
Fubini’s theorem allows downward sequential or successive int´egration.
Be careful. If the whole domain of integration is a rectangle, that is
for the form
D = D1 × D1 × ... × Dk−1 × Dk,
===== page 535 =====
8. FINITE PRODUCT MEASURE SPACE AND FUBINI’S THEOREM
525
and we compute this integral
Z
D1×D1×...×Dk−1×Dk
f(ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1, ωk) dm(ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1, ωk),
then, we still have Fubini’s theorem by replacing
Ω1 × Ω1 × ... × Ωk−1 × Ωk
by
D1 × D1 × ... × Dk−1 × Dk.
But if the integration domain D is not a rectangle with independent
factors, we have be cautious. In the ﬁrst step, we integrate on the
section
D(ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1) = {ωk ∈Ωk, (ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1, ωk) ∈D}.
Once this is done, the result is a function of (ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1) deﬁned
on the projection of D on Ω1 × Ω1 × ... × Ωk−1 that is
πk−1(D) = {(ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1), ∃ωk ∈Ωk, (ω1, ω2, ..., ωk−1, ωk) ∈D}.
And we proceed similarly downwards. Identifying the projections and
the sections depends on particular cases and require experience. You
certainly did a lot of that in undergraduate Calculus courses of on
multiple integration.

General conclusion and extension.
(a) The solutions of the exercise are summarized in Doc 07-01, which
usually constitutes the main course in ﬁnite product σ-ﬁnite measures.
We did not need any topological properties on the spaces Ωi, 1 ≤i ≤k,
to prove its existence. On topological spaces like Rd, it is possible to
derive the product measure of ﬁnite measures from the Caratheordory’s
Theorem by using the their topological properties, especially by using
the nice fact that on such spaces, ﬁnite measures are determined by
their values on compact sets. We will come back to such constructions
in the Exercises book to come later.
(b) (Extension). We want to close this document replacing the σ-
ﬁniteness by a more general condition. If we summarize all the points
discussed here, the σ-ﬁniteness intervenes to ensure the uniqueness of
the product measure and to show that :
(H1) For all A ∈A, the application ω1 7→m2(Aω1) is A1-measurable.
From this, all the other properties readily follow. This leads to this
extension.

9. σ-ADDITIVE SETS APPLICATIONS AND RADON-NIKODYM’S THEOREM
DOC 08-01 : Radon-Nikodym’s Theorem - A sum-
mary
Radon-Nikodym’s Theorem is very important in modern analysis. As
to probability theory, it is used for many purposes. But two of them
are certainly the most important:
(a) The probability density functions, with respect to the Lebesgue
measure or to the counting measure.
(b) The modern and general theory of conditional expectation which
is founded on this theorem.
The proof of this theorem is lengthy. You will be provided of it. But
the most important is to know where its comes and how it works. This
is what we will do now.
This documents put it in the general context of σ-additive applications.
Part I : σ-additive applications.
Let us consider a measurable space (Ω, A). To be c, the deﬁnition of
the σ-additivity of a real-valued application φ on A requires that φ
should not take both values −∞and +∞. We have to avoid one them
so that the relation φ(A + B) = φ(A) + φ(B) makes sense for any pair
of disjoint elements of A. By convention, we exclude the value −∞in
our exposition. But, the situation where the value +∞us exclude is
possible. In such a case, we need to adapt the results.
So, by proper application on A, we mean an application from A to
R ∪+∞which takes at least a ﬁnite value.
On top of σ-additive applications or measure (non-negative σ-additive
applications) deﬁned on (Ω, A), we have the following deﬁnitions. All
the σ-additive applications considered here are supposed to be proper
and deﬁned on (Ω, A).
===== page 555 =====
9. σ-ADDITIVE SETS APPLICATIONS AND RADON-NIKODYM’S THEOREM
545
(08.01a). A σ-additive application φ2 is continuous with respect to
another A σ-additive application φ1, denoted φ2 << φ1, if and only if :
∀A ∈A, (φ1(A) = 0) ⇒(φ2(A) = 0),
that is, any φ1-null set is a φ2-null set.
(08.01b). A σ-additive application φ2 is singular with respect to an-
other A σ-additive application φ1 if and only if :
(∃N ∈A : φ1(N) = 0) and (∀A ∈A, φ2(A ∩Nc) = 0).
Remark that in this situation, for all A ∈A, φ2(A) = φ2(A ∩Nc) and
φ1(A) = φ2(A ∩N).
(08.02) Properties of σ-additive applications.
.
(08.02a) Let φ be σ-additive on A. Let (An)n≥0 be a sequence of mu-
tually disjoint of elements of such that φ
P
n≥0 An

is ﬁnite. Then the
series P
n≥0 φ (An) is absolutely convergent.
(08.02b) Let φ be σ-additive on A. If A and B are two elements of A
such that A ⊂B. Then φ(A) is ﬁnite whenever φ(A). In particular, φ
is ﬁnite if φ(Ω) is.
If φ is a measure, it is non-decreasing and σ-sub-additive.
(08.02c) Let φ is σ-additive on A be a proper application on A.
(A) If φ is σ-additive, it is continuous in the following sense :
that φ is continuous in the following sense :
For any non-decreasing sequence (An)n≥0, for any non-increasing se-
quence (An)n≥0 for which there exists n0 ≥0 such that φ(An0),
φ( lim
n→+∞An) =
lim
n→+∞φ(An). (LIM)
Warning. The main diﬀerence with the continuity deﬁned in Point
(04.10) in Doc 04-01 (page 210) of Chapter 5 resides in the limit
limn→+∞φ(An).
For measures, this limit is monotone.
In the gen-
eral case, we know nothing about that.

9. σ-ADDITIVE SETS APPLICATIONS AND RADON-NIKODYM’S THEOREM
Terminology.
If Formula (LIM) holds for any non-decreasing se-
quence (An)n≥0, we say that φ is continuous below.
If it holds for
any non-increasing sequence (An)n≥0 for which one of the φ(An) is ﬁ-
nite, we say that φ is continuous above.
(B) Let φ be ﬁnitely additive and
either continuous from below,
or ﬁnite and continuous at ∅(from above),
then φ is σ-additive.
(08.03d) Let φ be σ-additive on A. There exists two measurable sets
C and D such that φ(C) = sup φ and φ(D) = inf φ.
A proper σ-additive application may be called as a signed measure for
the following Hahn-Jordan Theorem.
(08.02) (Hahn-Jordan Theorem). Let φ be a σ-additive application.
(A) There exists two measures φ1 and φ2 such
φ = φ1 −φ2.
(B) The measures φi, (i = 1, 2), are may taken as
φ1(A) = sup{φ(B), B ⊂A, B ∈A} and φ2(A) = −inf{φ(B), B ⊂A, B ∈A}.
(C) Besides, there exists D ∈A such that for all A ∈A
φ1(A) = φ(ADc) and φ2(A) = −φ(AD).

(08.05) The Indeﬁnite integral has the following properties :
(08.05a) φX is σ-additive.
(08.05b) φX is continuous with respect to m.
(08.05c) If m is σ-ﬁnite and X is a.e. ﬁnite, φX is σ-ﬁnite.

theorem.

Part III : Radon-Nikodym’s Theorem.
The Radon-Nikodym’s Theorem is the reverse of Part II. The ﬁrst re-
sult is referred to Lebesgue Decomposition Theorem.
(08.06) Let φ be a σ-additive application deﬁned on (Ω, A) and let m

φc deﬁned on A and m-continuous, and a diﬀerence of ﬁnite m-singular
and σ-additive application φs deﬁned on A such that
φ = φc + φs.

(Ω, A) 7→R such that φc is the indeﬁnite integral with respect X and

part in Formula (I02) is the null application.

Exercise 3.
===== page 580 =====
570
9. σ-ADDITIVE SETS APPLICATIONS AND RADON-NIKODYM’S THEOREM
Let φ be σ-additive on A. If A and B are two elements of A such that
A ⊂B. Show that φ(A) and φ(B \ A) are ﬁnite whenever φ(B) and
φ(B \ A) = φ(B) −φ(A).
In particular, φ is ﬁnite if φ(Ω) is.
Hint. Let A and B be two elements of A such that A ⊂B and φ(B)
is ﬁnite. Use the relationship φ(B) = φ(B \ A) + φ(A). Consider two
cases : the case where one of them is non-positive and the case where
both φ(B \ A) and φ(A) are both non-negative, and use again, when
necessary, the fact that the value −∞is excluded.
Be careful. φ(Ω) is not necessarily a maximum value for φ, while it
is if φ is non-negative.
SOLUTIONS.
Let us start with φ(B) = φ(B \A)+φ(A) with φ(B) ﬁnite and consider
two cases :
Case 1 : One φ(B \ A) and φ(A) is non-positive, say φ(B \ A) is non-
positive. Since φ does not take the value −∞, it is ﬁnite and then the
other term φ(A) is ﬁnite as the diﬀerence between two ﬁnite numbers.
Case 2 : Both φ(B \ A) and φ(A) are non-negative. Thus, they are
both ﬁnite since their sum is ﬁnite.
Finally if φ(Ω) is ﬁnite, φ is ﬁnite.

Question (d) Check (by two simple sentences) that φ1 and φ2 are mea-
sures. Is the application φ1 −φ2 well-deﬁned? Why?
Question (e) Conclude that φ = φ1 −φ2.
NB. If the value +∞is excluded in place of −∞, we establish the re-

SOLUTIONS.
The solution uses a σ-ﬁnite application which does not take the value
−∞. In the case where the value +∞is avoided, we apply the results
below to −φ and adapt the results.
Question (a) If φ is itself a measure, we have the decomposition φ1 = φ,
φ2 = 0 and D = ∅.
Question (b) Otherwise, there exists A0 ∈A such that φ(A0) < 0. By
Exercise 5 above, there exists D ∈A such that
−∞< inf φ = φ(D) ≤φ(A0) < 0.
Hence φ(D) is ﬁnite.
Now for any A ∈A, AD ⊂D. And, since φ(D) is ﬁnite, we have by
Exercise 3 :
φ(A \ AD) = φ(D) −φ(AD).
By the deﬁnition of the inﬁmum, the above formula implies that φ(AD) ≤
0. As well, we have by additivity
φ(D + ADc) = φ(D) + φ(ADc),
which, for the same reason, requires that φ(ADc) ≥0.
Question (c). Now, Let A ∈A.
(c1) We have
φ(ADc) ≤φ1(A) = sup{φ(A), B ⊂AandB ∈A},
since φ(ADc) is in the range over which the supremum is taken. Now
for all B ∈A with B ⊂A, we have

φ(B)
=

≤

≤

=
φ(ADc) by additivity.
By taking the supremum over B ⊂A, B ∈A, we get φ1(A) ≤φ(ADc).

(c2) We also that for A ∈A :

Exercise 2.
Let φ and ψ be two σ-additive applications which compatible in the
sense that the value −∞is excluded for both of them or the value +∞
is excluded for both of them, so that the application Φ = φ + ψ is
well-deﬁned and is σ-ﬁnite.
Question (a) Show that if φ and ψ are both m-continuous, then Φ is
m-continuous.
Question (b) Show that if φ and ψ are both m-singular measures,
then Φ is m-continuous.
Question (c) Show that if φ is both m-continuous and m-singular, then
φ is the null application.
SOLUTIONS.
Suppose that the value −∞is excluded for both of φ and ψ.
Question (a) Suppose that φ and ψ are both m-continuous. Thus, for
any A ∈A such that m(A) = 0, we have φ(A) = 0 and ψ(A) = 0 and
hence
Φ(A) = φ(A) + ψ(A) = 0.
Question (c) Suppose that φ and ψ are both m-singular. Thus, there
exist Ni ∈A such that
m(N1) = m(N2) = 0 and (∀A ∈A, φ(ANc
1) = ψ(ANc
2) = 0).
Set N = N1 ∪N2. We have N ∈N and m(N) = 0. But N1 ⊂N ⇒
Nc ⊂Nc
1. Hence, for any A ∈A,
0 ≤φ(ANc) ≤φ(ANc
1) = 0 and 0 ≤ψ(ANc) ≤ψ(ANc
2) = 0.
It follows that for any A ∈A, Φ(ANc) = 0. □.

Exercise on a continuous family of
measures
NB. This exercise is inspired by Lemma 2.1 and its proof in Shao
(2003), page 104. This result is important in Mathematical Statistics
regarding the factorization theorem for suﬃcient statistics.
Dominated family of σ-additives applications. Let M be a fam-
ily of real-valued σ-additive and non-null applications deﬁned on (Ω, A)
and let m be a measure deﬁned on the same space. For a coherence
purpose, let us suppose
The family M is dominated by m if and only any element of M is
m-continuous. The family M is dominated if and only if there is a
measure on (Ω, A) which dominates it.
NB. In all this document, M is a family of real-valued σ-additive appli-
cations deﬁned on (Ω, A) and m a measure deﬁned on the same space.
Summary. The objective of the exercise is to show this.
(A) If m is ﬁnite, A is dominated by a countable linear combination of
elements of its elements whose set of coeﬃcient is bounded.
(B) if m is σ-ﬁnite, M is dominated by a countable linear combination
of elements of M supported by m-ﬁnite subspaces and is associated to
a bounded set of coeﬃcients.

Exercise on a continuous family of

NB. This exercise is inspired by Lemma 2.1 and its proof in Shao

regarding the factorization theorem for suﬃcient statistics.
Dominated family of σ-additives applications. Let M be a fam-
ily of real-valued σ-additive and non-null applications deﬁned on (Ω, A)
and let m be a measure deﬁned on the same space. For a coherence
purpose, let us suppose
The family M is dominated by m if and only any element of M is
m-continuous. The family M is dominated if and only if there is a
measure on (Ω, A) which dominates it.
NB. In all this document, M is a family of real-valued σ-additive appli-
cations deﬁned on (Ω, A) and m a measure deﬁned on the same space.
Summary. The objective of the exercise is to show this.
(A) If m is ﬁnite, A is dominated by a countable linear combination of
elements of its elements whose set of coeﬃcient is bounded.
(B) if m is σ-ﬁnite, M is dominated by a countable linear combination
of elements of M supported by m-ﬁnite subspaces and is associated to
a bounded set of coeﬃcients.

10. INTRODUCTION TO SPACES Lp, p ∈[1, +∞] AND DANIEL INTEGRAL
DOC 09-01 : Lp Spaces - A summary
(09-01) Space Lp(Ω, A, m), p ∈[1, +∞[.
Lp(Ω, A, m) is the space of all real-valued and measurable functions f
deﬁned on Ωsuch that |f|p is integrable with respect to m, that is
Lp(Ω, A, m) = {f ∈L0(Ω, A)
Z
|f|p dm < +∞}.
Recall. L0(Ω, A) is the class of all real-valued and measurable functions
deﬁned on Ωas in Point (4.19a) in Doc 04-01 of Chapter 5, page 220.
(09-02) Space of m-a.e. bounded functions L∞(Ω, A, m).
L∞(Ω, A, m) is the class of all real-valued and measurable functions
which are bounded m-a.e., i.e,
L∞(Ω, A, m) = {f ∈L0(Ω, A)0, ∃C ∈R+, |f| ≤C m −a.e}.
(05-03) Equivalence classes.
When we consider only the equivalence classes pertaining to the equiv-
alence relation introduced in Point 4.19b in Doc 04-01 in Chapter 5,
page 210, we get the quotient spaces
Lp(Ω, A, m) = Lp(Ω, A, m)/R.
that is
Lp(Ω, A, m) = { ˙f, f ∈L0(Ω, A),
Z
|f|p dm < +∞}
and
L∞(Ω, A, m) = L+∞(Ω, A, m)/R,
that is
L∞(Ω, A, m) = { ˙f, fL0(Ω, A), |f| m −bounded}.

(09-04) Norms on Lp.
The space Lp(Ω, A, m), 1 ≤p < +∞, is equipped with the norm
||f||p = (
Z
|f|pdm)1/p.
The space L+∞(Ω, A, m) is equipped with the norm
||f||∞= inf{C ∈R+, |f| ≤C m −.a.e}.
(09-05) Remarkable inequalities on Lp.
09-05 Space L∞(Ω, A, m).
Deﬁne L
∞as the class of the equivalence classes of measurable functions
m-a.e. bounded. An other to clearly deﬁne it the following. Deﬁne
L∞= {f ∈L0, ∃M ∈[0, +∞[, |f| ≤M, m −a.e.}.
Next, deﬁne L∞= L/R as the quotient space of by the equivalence
relation R of the m-a.e. equality. For f ∈L∞, deﬁne
∥f∥∞= inf{M ∈[0, +∞[, |f| ≤M, m −a.e.}.
∥.∥∞is a norm and (L∞, +, ., ∥.∥∞) is a real Banach space.
(09-06) Banach spaces Lp, p ∈[1, +∞].
For all p ∈[1, +∞], Lp(Ω, A, m)(+, ., ||f||p) is a Banach space.
L2(Ω, A, m)(+, ., ||f||2) is a Hilbert space.
(09-07) Convergence in Lp.
(09-07a) Let (fn)n≥0 ⊂Lp, p ∈[1, +∞[, converge in Lp to f, denoted
by
fn
Lp
−→f.
===== page 636 =====
626
10. INTRODUCTION TO SPACES Lp, p ∈[1, +∞] AND DANIEL INTEGRAL
We have the following implications.
(a) (fn)n≥0 is Cauchy in measure.
(b) There exists a sub-sequence (fnk)k≥0 of (fn)n≥0 converging m-a.e.
and in measure to f.
(c) (fn)n≥0 converges in measure to f.
(d) ∥fn∥p converges to ∥f∥p.
(09-07b) Let (fn)n≥0 ⊂L∞converge in L∞to f. Then fn →f m-a.e.
NB. It is possible to reverse the implications, especially for 1 ≤p <
+∞, at the cost of extra conditions, among with equi-integrability ones,
for ﬁnite measures. This is why we reserve that part to the probability
theory monographs.

DOC 09-02 : Lp Spaces by Exercises
NB 1. Throughout this document (Ω, A, m) is a measure space. Func-
tions f, g, h, fn, gn, hn, n ≥0, etc., are classes of measurable functions
deﬁned on (Ω, A) with values in A.
Exercise 1. (Useful inequalities on real numbers)
Question (a) Let a and b be ﬁnite real numbers and let p ≥1 be ﬁnite
real number. Show that
|a + b|p ≤2p−1(|a|p + |b|p). (I1)
Hints. For p ≥1, use the convexity of the function ]0, +∞[∋x 7→xp.
Question (b) Two positive real numbers p and q are conjugate if and
only if 1/p + 1/q = 1. Show that p and q are conjugate, then both of
them are greater that the unity and that p = q/(q−1) and q = p/(p−1).
Question (c) Let a and b be ﬁnite real numbers and let p > 1 and p > 1
be p ≥1 be conjugate real numbers. Show that Show that for all ﬁnite

p

q . (I2)
Hints.
(a) Consider a ≥0 and b ≥0. Since Formula (I2) is obvious for ab = 0,
continue with a > 0 and b > 0. Use the development of (a −b)2 to
solve the case p = q.

metry of the roles, consider 1 < q < p. Show that : ap−1 > b and
bp−1 > a leads to a < a(q−1)/(p−1). Conclude that we have bp−1 ≤a or
ap−1 ≤b. In both cases we use the same method by making a and b
playing symmetrical roles.
Suppose that ap−1 ≤b.
The curve of the non-decreasing function

Exercise 3. (Minkowski’s Inequality)
Let p ≥1 and let that (f, g) ∈(Lp)2.

N∞(λf) = 0 and |λ|N∞(f) = 0.
If λ̸ = 0, the formula comes from the following identity :
inf{M ∈[0, +∞[, |λ||f| ≤M, m −a.e.}
=
inf{M ∈[0, +∞[, |f| ≤M/|λ|, m −a.e.}
=
|λ| inf{C ∈[0, +∞[, |f| ≤C, m −a.e.}.

(iii) Let (f, g) ∈L∞)2. Let us show that N∞(f +g) ≤N∞(f)+N∞(g).

that
0 ≤M1 <≤N∞(f) + ε/2 and |f| ≤M1, m −a.e.,
and

Hence for all ε > 0,
0 ≤M1 +M2 <≤N∞(f)+N∞(g)+ε and |f +g| ≤M1+M2, m−a.e.
By |f + g| ≤M1 + M2 m-a.e., we deduce that N∞(f + g) ≤M1 + M2
and thus for all ε > 0,
N∞(f + g) ≤N∞(f) + N∞(g) + ε.
We conclude by letting ε →0.
In conclusion N∞(.) = ∥.∥∞is a norm.

10. INTRODUCTION TO SPACES Lp, p ∈[1, +∞] AND DANIEL INTEGRAL
DOC 09-04 : Daniel Integral - Exercises
I - Introduction.
The Daniel-Stone integral is a kind of reverse of the following results.
Let (Ω, A, m) be a measure space. We have the following facts :
(L0) F = L1(Ω, A, m) is a lattice linear space, meaning that : it is a
linear space and it contains ﬁnite maxima and minima of its elements,
where the maximum and the minimum are relative to the order relation
on real-valued functions.
(L1) The functional
f ∋F 7→L(f) =
Z
f dm, (DS)
is linear and is non-negative, that if L(f) ≥0 for f ≥0.
(L2) For any sequence (fn)n≥0 ⊂F of non-decreasing to zero of non-
negative functions such that L(fn0) < +∞for some n0 ≥0, we have
lim
n→+∞L(fn) = 0.
If m is a probability measure we have in addition :
(L3) 1 ∈F and L(1) = 1.
The principle of the Daniel integral is to begin with a functional L on
a class of functions F satisfying a set of assumptions and to construct
a measure space (Ω, A, m) such that F ⊂L1(Ω, A, m) and L is of the
form (DS).
Several statements, which are more or less general, are available (See
for example Bogachev (2007b)). Here, we will restrict ourselves in the
case where m is a probability measure. Other forms are more or less
generalizations of the results here given.
This important construction of a measure plays an important role in
the theory of weak convergence of probability measures.
===== page 659 =====
10. INTRODUCTION TO SPACES Lp, p ∈[1, +∞] AND DANIEL INTEGRAL
649
II - Simple statement.
Let F be a lattice vector space of functions deﬁned on Ωwith values
in R and L be a linear form deﬁned on F such that the assumptions
(L1)-(L3) hold. Then there exists a unique probability measure m on
(Ω, AF) such that :
(i) F ⊂L1(Ω, A, m)
and
(ii) for all f ∈F
L(f) =
Z
f dm,
where A = AF) = σ(F)) is the σ-algebra generated by the class F,
that is generated by the class of subsets of Ω
CF = { f −1(B), f ∈F, B ∈B∞(R)}.
III - Construction of the Daniel integral by the exercise.
NB. These exercises are advanced ones. They greatly use the knowl-
edge of fundamental measure theory which is constituted by the con-
tents up to general Lp spaces. In particular, a number of methods used
in the study of elementary functions are re-conducted here.
In all the remaining part of the document, we suppose that the as-
sumptions of the statement in Part II hold.
Denote F + = {f ∈F, f ≥0}. Deﬁne L+ the class of all non-negative
and bounded real-valued functions deﬁned on Ωwhich are limits of
non-decreasing sequences of elements of F, that is
f ∈L+ ⇔f is bounded and ∃(fn)n≥0 ⊂F +, fn ր f as n ր +∞.

11. LEBESGUE-STIELJES MEASURES ON R4
DOC 10-01 : Lebesgue-Stieljes measure and Prob-
ability Theory
(10.01) Introduction.
A very large part of probability theory deals with real random variables,
random vectors or stochastic processes with real states. Usually, formal
and general spaces are used in theory but almost always, applications
come back to real data. The fundamental Theorem of Kolmogorov,
as we will see it in the course of stochastic process, will establish that
the probability law of an arbitrary family of real random variables is
characterized by the laws of its ﬁnite subfamilies.
This means that we ﬁnally work with probability laws on Rk. The point
is that probability laws in Rk are Lebesgue-Stieljes measures. This al-
lows us to say that mastering Lebesgue-Stieljes measures on Rk is the
key that opens the castle of Probability Theory.
Of course the very foundation of probability theory is measure theory
and its probability theory version. Normally, Lebesgue-Stieljes mea-
sures on Rk is part of the course of Measure theory. But the latter one
is so heavy that there is no enough space to handle it properly. From
a statistical point of view, the recent notion of copulas is now popular
and studying some areas of Statistics without copulas is impossible.
And it happens that copulas are special Lebesgue-Stieljes measures on
Rk.
Then it is of a great importance we provide a special cover of the
Lebesgue-Stieljes measures on Rk before we close this textbook.
Warning. We already have a ﬁrst and complete handling in the uni-
variate case in Doc 04-04, Doc 04-07 and Doc 04-10 (pages 241, 286
and 317). Although we will focus on the non-univariate case, we will
represent, once again, the univariate case for the reader who wants to
have all the materials in one document.
(10.02) Distribution functions and measures.
We are going to discover the deep relations between distribution func-
tions and measures on Rk assigning ﬁnite values to bounded above
===== page 675 =====
11. LEBESGUE-STIELJES MEASURES ON R4
665
Borel sets. The general case of arbitrary dimension k may make the
reader feel that it is complicated. So we are going to guide him by
beginning with the simplest case with k = 1. Next, we treat the in-
termediate case k = 2 and let him see principles that will guide the
general case. While dealing with complex expressions in the general
case, the meaning will be as simple as in the bivariate case.
(10.02a) Univariate case.
Let m be a measure on the Borel set of R such that for any any t ∈R,
Fm(t) = m(] −∞, t]) is ﬁnite. (RC01)
The function Fm : R 7−→R deﬁned by
t 7→Fm(t) (FD01)
has the following properties.
Claim 1. Fm assigns non-negative lengths to intervals, that is.,
t ≤s =⇒∆Fm(]t, s]) = Fm(s) −Fm(t) ≥0. (PI01)
Here, we have to pay attention to the terminology.
We say that
∆Fm(]t, s] = Fm(s) −Fm(t) is the length of the interval ]t, s] by Fm.
It is a pure coincidence that Formula (PI01) means that Fm is non-
decreasing in this particular case. In higher order spaces, this notion of
non-decreasingness will disappear while the notion of positive lengths
will be naturally extended to that positive areas and generally to pos-
itive volumes.
Now the proof of Formula (PI01) is immediately seen by remarking
that
(t ≤s) ⇒(] −∞, t]) ⊆] −∞, s])
and
m(]t, s]) = Fm(s) −Fm(t)
and then Formula (PI01) holds based on the fact that Fm(s) −Fm(t)
is the measure of an interval.
We say that Fm(s) −Fm(t) is the
length of ]t, s] by Fm or that Fm assigns to the interval ]t, s] the length
Fm(s) −Fm(t). With such a terminology, we are ready to move to area
in dimension two and to volumes in higher dimensions.
===== page 676 =====
666
11. LEBESGUE-STIELJES MEASURES ON R4
Claim 2. Fm is right-continuous, that is, for all t ∈R,
Fm(t(n)) ↓Fm(t)
as t(n)) ↓t.
Proof.
Let t(n)) ↓t. We get
] −∞, t(n))] =
↓] −∞, t].
Since m(] −∞, t(n))]) is ﬁnite for all n’s, we get by continuity of the
measure m, that
Fm(tn) = m(] −∞, tn]) ↓m(] −∞, t]) = Fm(t).
Suppose now that m is a probability measure, that is m(R) = 1, we
have two additional points.
Claim 3.
Fm(t) ↓0 as t ↓−∞and Fm(t) ↑1 as t ↑+∞.
These two points result from the continuity of the measure m. First
] −∞, t] ↓∅as t ↓−∞and m(] −∞, t]) < +∞for all t ∈R
implies
m(] −∞, t]) ↓m(∅) = 0 as t ↓−∞.
Next
] −∞, t] ↑R as t ↑+∞
implies
m(] −∞, t]) ↑m(R) = 0 as t ↑−∞.
In the sequel, we make these notation : F(−∞) = limt↓−∞F(t) and
F(+∞) = limt↑+∞F(t). We summarize our study through theses two
deﬁnitions.
===== page 677 =====
11. LEBESGUE-STIELJES MEASURES ON R4
667
Deﬁnition. A non-constant function F : R 7−→R is a distribution
function if and only if
(1) it assigns non-negative lengths to intervals
and
(2) it is right-continuous at any point t ∈R.
This deﬁnition is very broad. F is not necessarily non-negative. It is
not required that F(−∞) = 0. The second deﬁnition is more restrictive.
Deﬁnition. A non-negative function F : R 7−→R is a probability
distribution function, or a cumulative distribution function (cdf ), if
and only if the following assertions hold :
(1) it assigns non-negative lengths to intervals.
(2) it is right-continuous.
(3) F(−∞) = 0 and F(+∞) = 1.
We conclude by saying that a measure m which assigns ﬁnite values
to bounded above intervals generates the distribution functions Fm.
A probability measure generates the probability distribution functions
Fm.
Conversely, let be given a distribution function F. From the distribu-
tion function F + c,
it is possible to create a measure on the semi-
algebra
I1 = {]a, b], −∞≤a ≤b ≤+∞}
that generates the usual σ-algebra B(R) by
(0.1)
mF(]a, b]) = ∆F(]a, b]) = F(b) −F(a). (LS01)
Let us show that mF is additive on I1. The only possibility to have
that an element I1 of is sum of two elements of is to split on element
of into two others, that is
]a, b] =]a, c]+]c, b], where c is ﬁnite and a < c < b
===== page 678 =====
668
11. LEBESGUE-STIELJES MEASURES ON R4
and then
mF(]a, c]) + mF(]c, b])
=
F(c) −F(a) + F(b) −F(c)
=
F(b) −F(a) = mF(]a, b]).
Extension of mF to an additive application on the algebra C generated
by I1 which consists in the collection of ﬁnite sums of elements of I1,
still denoted as mF, is straightforward by simple arguments established
in the course of Measure Theory. mF is also σ-ﬁnite since
R =
[
n≥1
] −∞, n] and, ∀n ≥1, mF(] −∞, n]) < +∞.
It remains to show that mF is sigma-sub-additive on C. From there,
the Caratheodory’s Theorem ensures that mF is uniquely extensible to
a measure, still denoted as mF, on the usual sigma-algebra generated
both by I1 and by C.
The distribution function FmF generated by mF is obtained by letting
a go to −∞in (0.7) that gives
FmF (b) = mF(] −∞, b]) = F(b) −F(−∞),
which is exactly F if F(−∞) = 0. And, we have the following conclu-
sion.
Let M0 the class of measures m on R, such that
∀u ∈R, m(] −∞, u]) < +∞
and F0 the class of distribution functions F on R such that
F(−∞) = 0.
There is a one-to-one mapping between M0 and F0 in the following
way
F(u) = m(] −∞, u])
←→
m(]a, b]) = ∆F(a, b).
In particular, there is a one-to-one mapping between the class P of
probability measures on R and the class FP of probability distribution
functions on R. Probability measures are characterized by their distri-
bution functions.

Rule of forming ∆F(a, b). First consider F(b1, b2) the value of the
distribution function at the right endpoint b = (b1, b2) of the interval

the formed points, with a sign plus (+) if the number of replacements
is even and with a sign minus (−) if the number of replacements is odd.

Existence Theorem of Lebesgue-Stieljes Measures.
Let F be a distribution function on Rk, then the application mF that

F(t) = mF(]∞, t]), t ∈Rk.
If F is a probability distribution function, then mF is a probability
measure.
This theorem is announced in any of the three parts. It constitutes the
foundation of the theory exposed here. But the reader does not neces-
sarily need it to understand Lebesgue-Stieljes measures and its many
usages. So, we postpone in the next document where fundamentalist
mathematicians will ﬁnd it.

DOC 10-03 : Functions of bounded variation
Later and hopefully, the reader will study Stochastic Calculus. The ori-
gin of such a theory is related to the notion of bounded variation. We
will shortly see that the Riemann-Stieljes related to a function F are
well-deﬁned if F is of bounded variation. Actually, we will show that
of function of bounded variation is diﬀerence between two monotone
functions and this makes possible the extension of the Lebesgue-Stieljes
integral with respect to a bounded-variation function.
Since the Brownian motion B is non-where of bounded variation, inte-
grating with respect to dBt, path-wisely, in the Stieljes sense does not
make sense. This gave birth to stochastic integration.
Let us begin by the concept of total variation.
A - Bounded variation functions.
Let F : ]a, b] −→R be ﬁnite function. Consider a subdivision π = (a =
x0 < x1 < ... < xℓ< xℓ+1 = b) of [a, b]. The variation of F on [a, b]
over π is deﬁned by
V (F, [a, b], π) =
ℓ−1
X
i=0
|F(xi+1) −F(xi+1)| .
Denote by P([a, b]) the set of all subdivisions of [a, b]. We may deﬁne
Deﬁnition. The total variation V (F, [a, b]) of F on [a, b] is the supre-
mum of its variation over all the subdivisions of [a, b], that is
V (F, [a, b]) =
sup
π∈P([a,b])
V (F, [a, b], π).
The function F is of bounded variation on [a, b] if and only V (F, [a, b])
is ﬁnite.
Examples.
Let F be monotonic on [a, b] , we easily see that any
variation of F on is exactly |F(a) −F(b)| . To set that, let F be non-
decreasing. We have
V (F, [a, b], π) =
m
X
i=0
F(xi+1) −F(xi)
===== page 701 =====
11. LEBESGUE-STIELJES MEASURES ON R4
691
= F(x1) −F(x0) + F(x2) −F(x1) + ... + F(xℓ+1) −F(xℓ)
= F(xℓ+1) −F(x0) = F(a) −F(b).
If F is non-increasing, apply thus result to −F. We have this statement
:
Monotonic functions F on [a, b] are of bounded variation.
It is remarkable that non-decreasing functions characterize bounded
variation functions in the following way.
Proposition. Any bounded variation F on [a, b] is a diﬀerence of two
non-decreasing functions.
Proof. Let a ≤x < y ≤b. Pick subdivision π1 ∈P([a, x]) and a
subdivision π2 ∈P([x, y]). It is clear that we get a subdivision π of
[a, y] by binding π1 and π2 at the point x and
V (F, [a, y], π) = V (F, [a, x], π1) + V (F, [x, y], π2).
Since V (F, [a, y], π) ≤V (F, [a, y]), we get
V (F, [a, x], π1) + V (F, [x, y], π2) ≤V (F, [a, y]).
Since π1
and π2 are arbitrary elements of P([a, x]) and P([x, y]), we
may get the suprema to get
(0.7)
V (F, [a, x]) + V (F, [x, y]) ≤V (F, [a, y]).
Put
F1(x) = V (F, [a, x]), a ≤x ≤b.
We see by 0.7 that for a ≤x < y ≤b
F1(y) ≥F1(x) + V (F, [x, y]) ≥F1(x),
since V (F, [x, y]). Then F1 is non-decreasing. Let
F2(x) = F1(x) −F(x).
===== page 702 =====
692
11. LEBESGUE-STIELJES MEASURES ON R4
Let a ≤x < y ≤b. We have
F2(y) −F2(x) = (F1(y) −F1(x)) −(F(y) −F(x)).
But F(y) −F(x) is a variation of F over [x, y] with the subdivision
π = (x0 = x < x1 = y). Then F(y) −F(x) ≤V (F, [x, y]). So
F2(y) −F2(x) = (F1(y) −F1(x)) −V (F, [x, y]),
which is non-negative by0.7. So F2 is also non-decreasing and
F = F1 −F2.
We easily remark that if F is right-continuous, we apply h ↓0 to
F(x + h) = F1(x + h) −F2(x + h),
to get
F(x) = F1(x+) −F2(x+).
where Fi(x+) is the right-limit of Fi(x) that exists since each Fi, i =
1, 2, is monotonic. We also know that the functions x −→Fi(x+),
i = 1, 2, are right-continuous. We get
Proposition. Any right-continuous and bounded variation function on
[a, b] is diﬀerence of two right-continuous and non-decreasing function.
B - Existence of the Riemann-Stieljes integrals.
Now we are going to prove this.
Theorem. Let F be of bounded variation on ]a, b]. Any continuous f
on ]a, b] is Riemann-Stieljes is integrable.
The proof that follows cannot certainly be given in undergraduate
courses.
It uses the additivity properties of λF on I1 and next on
C = a(I1).
Remark that neither right-continuity nor monotonicity
does play a role on ﬁnite additivity. The ﬁrst condition only intervenes
for σ-additivity and the second for the sign of λF and its monotonicity.
We do not deal with these concepts in this particular section.

Before we turn back to the Riemann-Stieljes integration, just remark
that Riemann-Stieljes sums satisfy
Sn(f, F, a, b, πn, cn) = Tn(fn, πn, dn(f)),
where

With this notations, we may show that sequence Sn(f, F, a, b, πn, cn) is
Cauchy.

Deﬁnition ℓ∈R is an accumulation point of a sequence (xn)n≥0 of real
numbers ﬁnite or inﬁnite, in R, if and only if there exists a sub-sequence
(xn(k))k≥0 of (xn)n≥0 such that xn(k) converges to ℓ, as k →+∞.

Exercise 4. (Convergence criteria)
Prohorov Criterion Let (xn)n≥0 be a sequence of R and a real number
ℓ∈R such that: Every subsequence of (xn)n≥0 also has a subsequence
( that is a subssubsequence of (xn)n≥0 ) that converges to ℓ. Then, the
limit of (xn)n≥0 exists and is equal ℓ.
Upcrossing or Downcrossing Criterion.
Let (xn)n≥0 be a sequence in R and two real numbers a and b such that
a < b. We deﬁne
ν1 =

inf
{n ≥0, xn < a}
+∞
if (∀n ≥0, xn ≥a) .
If ν1 is ﬁnite, let
ν2 =

inf
{n > ν1, xn > b}
+∞
if (n > ν1, xn ≤b) .
.
As long as the ν′
js are ﬁnite, we can deﬁne for ν2k−2(k ≥2)
ν2k−1 =

inf
{n > ν2k−2, xn < a}
+∞
if (∀n > ν2k−2, xn ≥a) .
and for ν2k−1 ﬁnite,
ν2k =

inf
{n > ν2k−1, xn > b}
+∞
if (n > ν2k−1, xn ≤b) .
We stop once one νj is +∞. If ν2j is ﬁnite, then
xν2j −xν2j−1 > b −a.
We then say : by that moving from xν2j−1 to xν2j, we have accomplished
a crossing (toward the up) of the segment [a, b] called up-crossings. Sim-
ilarly, if one ν2j+1 is ﬁnite, then the segment [xν2j, xν2j+1] is a crossing
downward (down-crossing) of the segment [a, b]. Let
D(a, b) = number of up-crossings of the sequence of the segment [a, b].

Exercise 3. Let (xn)n≥0 be a non-decreasing sequence, we have:
zn = sup
p≥n
xp = sup
p≥0
xp, ∀n ≥0.
Why? Because by increasingness,
{xp, p ≥0} = {xp, 0 ≤p ≤n −1} ∪{xp, p ≥n} .
Since all the elements of {xp, 0 ≤p ≤n −1} are smaller than than
those of {xp, p ≥n} , the supremum is achieved on {xp, p ≥n} and so
ℓ= sup
p≥0
xp = sup
p≥n
xp = zn.
Thus
zn = ℓ→ℓ.
We also have yn = inf {xp, 0 ≤p ≤n} = xn, which is a non-decreasing
sequence and so converges to ℓ= sup
p≥0
xp.
Exercise 4.

point.
(xnk)k≥1 ⊆(xn)n≥0 such that xnK →ℓ′.
By hypothesis this sub-sequence (xnK) has in turn a sub-sub-sequence

xn(k(p))

p≥1 such that xn(k(p)) →ℓas p →+∞.
But as a sub-sequence of

xn(k)

,
xn(k(ℓ)) →ℓ′.
Thus
ℓ= ℓ′.

Applying that to the limit superior and limit inferior, we have:
lim xn = lim xn = ℓ.
And so lim xn exists and equals ℓ.
Exercise 5.
Question (a). If ν2k is ﬁnite and if ν2k+1 is inﬁnite, then there ate
exactly k up-crossings : [xν2j−1, xν2j], j = 1, ..., k, that is, we have
D(a, b) = k.
Question (b).
If ν2k+1 is ﬁnite and ν2k+2 is inﬁnite, then there
are exactly k up-crossings: [xν2j−1, xν2j], j = 1, ..., k, that is we have
D(a, b) = k.
Question (c). If all the ν′
js are ﬁnite, then there are an inﬁnite num-
ber of up-crossings : [xν2j−1, xν2j], j ≥1k : D(a, b) = +∞.
Question (d). Suppose that there exist a < b rationals such that
D(a, b) = +∞. Then all the ν′
js are ﬁnite. The subsequence xν2j−1 is
strictly below a. So its limit inferior is below a. This limit inferior is
an accumulation point of the sequence (xn)n≥1, so is more than lim xn,
which is below a.
Similarly, the subsequence xν2j is strictly below b. So the limit superior
is above a. This limit superior is an accumulation point of the sequence
(xn)n≥1, so it is below lim xn, which is directly above b. This leads to :
lim xn ≤a < b ≤lim xn.
That implies that the limit of (xn) does not exist. In contrary, we just
proved that the limit of (xn) exists, meanwhile for all the real numbers
a and b such that a < b, D(a, b) is ﬁnite.
Now, suppose that the limit of (xn) does not exist. Then,
lim xn < lim xn.
We can then ﬁnd two rationals a and b such that a < b and a number
ǫ such that 0 < ǫ, such that
lim xn < a −ǫ < a < b < b + ǫ < lim xn.
If lim xn < a −ǫ, we can return to Question (a) of Exercise 2 and con-
struct a sub-sequence of (xn) which tends to lim xn while remaining
===== page 720 =====
710
12. GENERAL APPENDIX
below a−ǫ. Similarly, if b+ǫ < lim xn, we can create a sub-sequence of
(xn) which tends to lim xn while staying above b+ ǫ. It is evident with
these two sequences that we could deﬁne with these two sequences all
νj ﬁnite and so D(a, b) = +∞.
We have just shown by contradiction that if all the D(a, b) are ﬁnite
for all rationals a and b such that a < b, then, the limit of (xn)n≥0 exists.
Exercise 5. Cauchy criterion in R.
Suppose that the sequence is Cauchy, i.e.,
lim
(p,q)→(+∞,+∞) (xp −xq) = 0.
Then let xnk,1 and xnk,2 be two sub-sequences converging respectively
to ℓ1 = lim xn and ℓ2 = lim xn. So
lim
(p,q)→(+∞,+∞) (xnp,1 −xnq,2) = 0.
, By ﬁrst letting p →+∞, we have
lim
q→+∞ℓ1 −xnq,2 = 0,
which shows that ℓ1 is ﬁnite, else ℓ1 −xnq,2 would remain inﬁnite and
would not tend to 0. By interchanging the roles of p and q, we also
have that ℓ2 is ﬁnite.
Finally, by letting q →+∞, in the last equation, we obtain
ℓ1 = lim xn = lim xn = ℓ2.
which proves the existence of the ﬁnite limit of the sequence (xn).
Now suppose that the ﬁnite limit ℓof (xn) exists. Then
lim
(p,q)→(+∞,+∞) (xp −xq) = ℓ−ℓ= 0,
0 which shows that the sequence is Cauchy.

13. CONCLUSION AND BIBLIOGRAPHY
DOC 12-01 : Conclusion
The learner who went through all the material is expected to have cov-
ered a serious and solid part of measure theory. In the lines below, we
will see where this takes him.
(1) Without express it properly, a large part of fundamental Probabil-
ity theory is already acquired with the proper terminology : Normed
measures are probability measures, measurable functions are random
variables, measurable sets are events, almost everywhere (a.e.) prop-
erties are almost-sure (a.s.) ones. Here are some important facts.
(2) The probability law of a random element is the image-measure of
the underlying probability measure by this random element.
(3) The mathematical expectation with respect to a probability mea-
sure of a real-valued random variable is nothing else than its integral
if it exists.
(4) The independence of two random elements means that their joint
probability laws is the product measure of their probability laws.
(5) The Radon-Nikodym’s Theorem and the results of Lp spaces, as
well as the handling of absolutely convergent series as integral with re-
spect to counting measures are very useful for the learner in the course
of Functional Analysis. If this course has been taken before Measure
Theory, a re-reading of the ﬁrst course ensure him a deeper under-
standing.
(6) Also, the The Radon-Nikodym’s Theorem is the more general tool
for dealing with the conditional expectation of a real-valued X random
variable with respect to a sub-σ-algebraB.
It is simply the Radon-
Nikodym derivative of the indeﬁnite integral with respect to X deﬁned
on B.
The theory of mathematical expectation is the general frame to deal
with the main dependent sequence or stochastic processes as martin-
gales, Markov chains or Markov stochastic processes, etc.
(7) Another approach of handling the conditional mathematical expec-
tation is to considered as a projection in Hilbert spaces. This will be
===== page 723 =====
13. CONCLUSION AND BIBLIOGRAPHY
713
considered in Functional Analysis.
(8) The fundamental law of Kolmogorov is the generalization of the
ﬁnite product measure to the inﬁnite (countably or not) case. This
generalization needs topological properties to hold.
(9) Specially, Documents Doc 08-06 and Doc 08-07 have been intro-
duced as a key tool for Mathematical Statistics in relation with the
Factorization Theorem for suﬃcient statistics.
(10) Mathematical Statistics is largely based on Measure Theory, es-
pecially on probability laws.
(11) The Daniel integral studied in Doc 09-04 is the master key to
establish the convergence of stochastic processes.
These are only a sample of remarkable facts we provided just to en-
lighten the power of measure theory and its impact of a a considerable
number of Mathematics ﬁelds.
I ﬁnish by advising the learner to have as many readings of the book
as possible to the point he is able to repeat the whole content himself.
